### 40. The Spirit, Not the Letter, Must Speak Through the Teacher of Religion.

The Spirit of Christ in His children is bound to no specific form, as if He were no longer allowed to speak anything that was not found in the apostolic letter <note>The "Letter" (*der Buchstabe*) refers to the literal, written text of the Bible, contrasted here with the "living" Spirit.</note>. The Spirit in the apostles was likewise free; they did not all speak the same words, but they all spoke from one Spirit and Foundation, each as the Spirit gave him utterance. Thus, even now, the Spirit of Christ still speaks through His children and requires no formula composed in advance from the literal word. He Himself reminds the human spirit of what is contained within the letter.

No one is Christ’s shepherd who does not have Christ’s Spirit and teach from Him. Neither human skill <note>original: *Kunst*. In this era, this refers to academic training, rhetoric, or technical mastery of theology.</note> nor university makes him a shepherd of Christ, unless he is capable of the office in the Spirit of Christ. If he does not have that Spirit alive and working within him, then the people have chosen for themselves only a bungler <note>original: *Schnitzer*. Literally a "woodcarver" or "whittler," used here to mean someone who clumsily carves out meanings without inspiration.</note> and a peddler of letters <note>original: *Buchstabenwechsler*. Someone who merely swaps or manipulates literal words without understanding their spiritual weight.</note>, without divine understanding and knowledge; for how will such a person teach the way of God when he understands nothing of it himself? But they say that the written word is the voice of Christ; yes, it is indeed the housing <note>original: *Gehäuse*. The outer casing or shell of an object.</note>, as a form of the Word, but the voice must be alive, which drives the housing like a clockwork <note>The author uses a mechanical metaphor popular in the 17th and 18th centuries: the Bible is the beautiful outer case of a clock, but the Spirit is the internal mechanism that actually makes it move and tell time.</note>.

<vocab>Spirit of Christ, Letter, Teacher of Religion, Apostle, Shepherd of Christ, Formula, Knowledge, Clockwork</vocab>

<summary>This page argues that true religious teaching must come from the living Spirit of Christ rather than mere academic study or literal adherence to the Bible. It compares the written word to the "housing" of a clock that requires the "voice" of the Spirit to function.</summary>
<keywords>Spirit vs. Letter, spiritual authority, apostles, religious education, internal vs. external word, pastoral qualifications, clockwork metaphor</keywords>

--- Page 150 ---
<meta>This page continues the metaphor of the "Letter" versus the "Spirit" from the previous page. While the previous section critiqued those who merely "peddle letters," this page explains that the written word is only a tool that requires a spiritually awakened person to "play" it correctly.</meta>

The literal letter is an instrument for this purpose, like a trumpet; but a true resonance <note>original: *Hall*. Refers to the sound or echo that gives life to the instrument.</note> must belong within it, one that matches the resonance found in the letter. The word of the letter is a prepared tool <note>original: *zugerichtetes Werk*. Literally a "prepared work" or "fashioned object," like a musical instrument ready to be played.</note>. Whatever kind of trumpet-blower enters into it, that is how it sounds.

Whoever wishes to ring the little inner bell <note>original: *Glöcklein*. A recurring mystical metaphor for the soul's inner voice or the spark of divinity.</note> that is locked away in death must also possess the clapper that shatters death, so that the bell may ring out; for the divine sound was locked in death within Adam <note>Refers to the theological concept of the Fall of Man, where humanity's original spiritual connection was lost or silenced.</note>. Now, if anyone wishes to awaken that sound, he must have that same sound alive within himself. He must speak from Christ’s Spirit and voice, or else his teaching is merely shadow-boxing <note>original: *Spiegelgefechten*. Fighting with reflections; empty or superficial effort without a real opponent.</note>, and he is nothing but a "belly-servant" <term>Bauchdiener: someone who serves their own physical needs, status, or social habits rather than God</term> of habit, and of no use to the temple of Christ; for he is not within it himself—how then does he intend to lead others into it? Therefore, preaching from mere academic skill <note>original: *Kunst*. In this era, this refers to intellectual or rhetorical training, often synonymous with university theology without personal faith.</note> is nothing but vain deception; such skill awakens no one who lies locked in death. There must be a true hammer to strike the dead bell and make it ring.

For the temple of Christ is within us; we must learn to hear Christ teaching within us. If He does not teach within us, then the external shouting of the mouth is in vain and spoken only into the air. ### 41. New Teachings and Views.

Since so many different teachings and opinions are now revealing themselves, the mocker should not immediately rush in and throw everything into one pile just because he does not understand it; for not everything is false. Much has been born through Heaven, which shows itself most highly with its power, and now intends to create a new era <note>original: *Zeitalter*. This suggests a "Third Age" or a coming spiritual reformation, a common theme in 17th-century radical pietism.</note>.

No; he merely sifts away the impurities and uses the good seed for his nourishment; the chaff, however, he gives to his livestock, and with the stalks he fertilizes his field, so that everything must be useful to him. 

Go into a meadow and look at the herbs and flowers, all of which grow from the earth; while one herb may be more beautiful and fragrant than another, often the most despised plant possesses the greatest virtue <term>Tugend: in this context, the term refers to the "hidden power," "medicinal potency," or "innate quality" of a plant rather than just moral character</term>. In this way their power grew, and everyone looked toward them. They constantly strengthened their authority through the "Power of Saint Peter," until they seated themselves so high that they shamelessly raised themselves as lords even over God and the teachings of the Apostles. They claimed that the Word of God and the teachings of the saints must receive their value from their Council, and that whatever they established, God also did—for they claimed to be God’s Vicars of the Word.

A critical footnote by a later commentator questions the rational validity and religious importance of Jacob Böhme’s specific explanations of these mysteries.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the author's appeal for humility in the reader, transitioning from the general revelation of divine mysteries to the specific internal experience required to understand his work, Aurora.</meta>

Therefore, I wish to faithfully warn the reader to read this book \*) with diligence and not to take offense at the author's <term>simplicity<gloss>original: "Einfalt"; here meaning a lack of formal scholarly training or sophisticated rhetoric, which Böhme viewed as a spiritual advantage.</gloss></term>. For God does not look upon what is high <note>Referring back to the "heights" of academic and social status mentioned on the previous page; Böhme emphasizes that God ignores worldly prestige.</note>, for He alone is high; rather, He looks to see how He may help the lowly. Should it come to pass that you grasp the author's spirit and mind, then no further admonition will be necessary; instead, you will rejoice and be glad in this light, and your soul will laugh and triumph within it. For how could there not be love and joy where life is born in the midst of death, and light in the midst of darkness? 

You may ask: how does this happen? Indeed, if my spirit were seated in your heart and welled up within your heart, then your very body would experience and comprehend it. But I cannot bring it into your mind in any other way, nor can you comprehend it, unless the Holy Spirit ignites your soul so that this light shines within your heart itself. Then this light is born within you just as it is in God; it rises up out of your <term>harsh and bitter quality<gloss>original: "herben und bittern Qualität"; the initial "source-spirits" in Böhme's system that represent the tension and friction of existence before they are transformed by divine light.</gloss></term> into your <term>sweet water<gloss>original: "süßen Wasser"; a Böhmean term for the gentle, nourishing element of divine love and light that yields a state of peace.</gloss></term>, and it triumphs just as it does in God. When this happens, only then will you understand my book, and not before.

### 46. Eternal, Supersensible Being, Willing, and Creating of God.

The <term>Ungrund<gloss>original: "Ungrund"; Böhme’s term for the absolute, "groundless" nothingness or "abyss" that precedes the existence of God or the world.</gloss></term> is an eternal nothing, but makes an eternal beginning, as a <note>The sentence ends abruptly here as the main text is interrupted by a long footnote.</note>

---

<note>The following text appears to be a lengthy footnote or commentary keyed to the main text above.</note>

*) God in and of Himself, as a supersensible, incomprehensible being existing beyond time and space, is called a "Nothing" in this regard by Böhme. In this same respect, he also calls God the Abyss, the <term>Ungrund</term>, Eternity, the <term>Great Mystery<gloss>original Latin: "Mysterium Magnum"; the undifferentiated source or "chaos" from which all things arise.</gloss></term>, the Center, the Principle, and Magic—even the power towards essence. Yet he seeks not only to designate the highest being outside the world with these profound names, but also undertakes, with uncommon keenness of mind, to explain the supersensible, eternal being, working, willing, and knowing of the Deity. The first eternal beginning of the will in the "Nothing"—as the highest being—he calls a <term>longing<gloss>original: "Sucht"; in Böhme’s specific vocabulary, this is not an "addiction" but an intense, primordial craving or drive that initiates the movement from nothingness toward manifestation.</gloss></term>, which turns the will into "Something"; that is, where the highest being is conscious of its eternal being, working, willing, and knowing from eternity. He now calls the will that has reached consciousness a <term>Magus<gloss>Latin for "Magician"; here representing the divine will as a creative, conscious power.</gloss></term> who has found a "Something" within its mother—the Nothing, which is the capacity for the will—namely itself and the capacity for willing. Because the will, in its consciousness, [looks] upon the capacity of the will—that is, upon its <term>causality<gloss>original: "Causalität"; the principle of cause and effect through which the divine will begins to act.</gloss></term>

<summary>This page explores Jacob Böhme’s complex concept of the "Ungrund," the primordial nothingness that precedes God’s self-awareness and the creation of the universe. It describes the process by which the divine will awakens from a "Nothing" into a conscious "Something" through a primordial longing.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the exposition of Jacob Böhme’s theosophical system, specifically focusing on the metaphysical transition from the "Nothing" to "Something" through the mechanics of the Will and "Sucht" (longing). It follows the previous page's definition of the Ungrund.</meta>

<term>Longing<gloss>original: "Sucht"; in Böhme’s lexicon, this refers to a primordial, driving urge or "craving" that initiates creation.</gloss></term>, for the Nothing is a longing for Something. This is the eternal primal state of Magic, which in itself makes Something out of Nothing, since the longing is also a Nothing, being merely a will. It has Nothing, and is also Nothing that might give it Something, and it also has no place where it might find itself or lie down. So, if there is a longing in the Nothing, it makes itself the will to Something. This will is a spirit, like a thought, which proceeds from the longing and is the seeker of the longing; for it finds its mother—the longing—in which it now has a place of residence.

The will is therefore a spirit, and something other than the desiring longing (or the essence and power of the will). For the will is an unperceiving and unrecognizable life, but the longing is [interrupted] by the

When one considers how simply and with how few words Böhme seeks to represent the <term>supersensible<gloss>original: "Uebersinnliche"; that which exists beyond the reach of the physical senses.</gloss></term> in the highest being, one must admire his sharp intellect, and regret that his deep philosophical insights are everywhere obscured and, as it were, drowned by fantastic representations. <note>The commentator here expresses a common 19th-century critique of Böhme: praising his psychological/philosophical depth while dismissing his complex mystical imagery as "fantastic."</note>

### 47. What is God in himself, apart from Nature and the Creature?

God is the eternal unity, as the immeasurable and singular Good, which has nothing behind or before it that might give Him something, or contribute to Him, or that might move Him. He is without origin of time, in himself only One, as a <term>pure purity<gloss>original: "lautere Lauterkeit"; this refers to an absolute, unmixed state of being, free from any material or secondary elements.</gloss></term>, without contact <note>The original "Berührung" implies that God in His absolute essence is untouched by the friction, suffering, or limitations of the material world.</note>. He needs no place for his dwelling anywhere, but is at once outside the world and in the world, and is deeper than a thought might soar; indeed, if one spoke numbers for a hundred thousand years in succession regarding his greatness and depth, one would still not have even begun to express his depth, for he is infinity.

And it is not as <term>Reason<gloss>original: "Vernunft"; Böhme often uses this to mean human logic or intellectual pride, which struggles to understand spiritual mysteries.</gloss></term> believes—that God dwells alone above the stars, outside the location of this world. No place is prepared for Him where He might dwell specifically; rather, His revelation is simply varied. He is in, with, and through us, and where He becomes active in a life through His love, there He is manifest in His effect; there He is outflowing, willing, and perceptible. There He has made Himself a place, as in the ground of the soul, in the eternal idea of the eternal willing.

<note>This passage emphasizes the "immanence" of God—the idea that the divine is not a distant ruler in the sky, but a presence that "outflows" into the very center of human consciousness when a person is moved by love.</note>

**Vocabulary:**
God, Nature, Creature, Unity, Infinity, Revelation, Soul, Eternal Idea

<meta>This page continues the exploration of Jacob Böhme’s mystical theology. If you wish to know where God dwells, then take away nature and the <term>creature<gloss>original: "Creatur"; in this context, it refers to any created thing or being, including humans, animals, and the physical world.</gloss></term>; then God is everything. Within Him there is no <term>counsel<gloss>original: "Rathschlag"; this refers to deliberation or the process of making a plan. Böhme argues that God does not need to "think things over" because He is already the source of all action.</gloss></term>, for He Himself is the Unity and the <term>ground<gloss>original: "Grund"; the underlying foundation or cause of all existence.</gloss></term> of all things. Nature and the creature arise from Him; why then would He take counsel with Himself, since there is no enemy before Him nor behind Him, and He alone is everything: the willing, the ability, and the power? But we humans can see nothing more of the Spirit of God in eternity than the radiance of His majesty, for we feel His glorious power within us; it is our life and it leads us.

The eternal divine understanding is a free will, not arisen from something or through something; it is its own seat, and dwells solely and alone within itself. It is a single will, and is neither near nor far, neither high nor low, but it is everything, even though it appears to our senses as a <note>Böhme frequently uses "Nothing" (Nichts) to describe God’s essence—not as a void, but as something so beyond physical "thing-ness" that it cannot be perceived by the five senses.</note> Nothing.

### 48. The Unfathomable Origin of the Soul.

This is the greatest wonder that eternity has wrought: that it has created the Eternal into a bodily spirit <note>The "bodily spirit" refers to the human soul, which Böhme sees as a piece of the eternal divine essence housed within a physical form.</note>, which no <term>reason<gloss>original: "Vernunft"; Böhme uses this to describe human logical intellect, which he believes is limited and cannot grasp spiritual mysteries without divine illumination.</gloss></term> grasps and no sense finds, and which is unfathomable to us. For no spirit can fathom itself; it indeed sees its depth down into the ab- <note>The text cuts off here, likely intended to be "Abgrund" or "Abyss," a central concept in Böhme’s work representing the bottomless nature of the divine.</note>

## 49. Creation of the Sun, Mars, and Jupiter.

Just as the <term>Godhead<gloss>original: "Gottheit"; refers to the essential divine nature or the triune God in His eternal state.</gloss></term> is the power and the light of Paradise in the <term>second principle<gloss>In Böhme’s cosmology, the "Second Principle" is the world of Light and Love, distinct from the "First Principle" (the dark fire-world of God's wrath) and the "Third Principle" (our visible, material world).</gloss></term>, so also is the Sun the power and the light of this material world in the third principle. And as the Godhead shines in the eternal darkness in the first principle, so the Sun shines in the darkness in the third principle. And as the Godhead is the eternal power and the spirit of eternal life, so the Sun is the power and the spirit in this <note>The author uses "fragile" to emphasize the temporary and mortal nature of our physical existence compared to the eternal life of the spirit.</note> fragile life.

Now, a spirit is nothing other than an ascending will, and within the will is the <term>anguish<gloss>original: "Aengstlichkeit"; Böhme uses this term to describe the intense, contractive tension or "birth pangs" of desire that must occur before the "light" or spiritual consciousness can break through.</gloss></term> for birth; and in that anguish the fire is born, and in the fire the light, and through the light the will becomes friendly, lovely, gentle, and sweet; and in the sweet will the power is born, and out of the power is born the kingdom and the glory. Thus, the light retains the power; where it is extinguished, there the power and glory cease, and the kingdom also.

God, who is the eternal light, is the eternal will; He shines in the darkness, and the darkness has <note>A likely reference to John 1:5: "The light shines in the darkness, and the darkness has not overcome [or understood] it."</note> seized the will. In this, the anguish arises, and in the harsh anguish, the fire; and in the fire, the light; and out of the light, the power; and out of the power, the...

<summary>Böhme explains the metaphysical relationship between the Sun and the Godhead, describing the Sun as the spiritual heart of the material world. He details the "birth" of spiritual qualities—fire, light, and power—from the tension of the divine will.</summary>

### 50. The Creation.

Creation is nothing other than a revelation of the all-present, unfathomable God. Everything that He is in His eternal, beginningless birthing <note>original: "Gebärung"; refers to God's continuous process of self-manifestation and generation within the divine Trinity.</note>, of that also is the creation; yet not in the [same degree of] omnipotence and power, but rather as an apple grows on a tree—it is not the tree itself, but it grows from the power of the tree. Thus all things have sprung from divine desire and were created into a <term>being<gloss>original: "Wesen"; can mean essence, substance, or a living entity.</gloss></term>, where in the beginning no being was present for it.

### 55. Unity and Disunity in the Will.

A thing that grows out of a beginning has a beginning and an end, and does not grow higher than the <term>number</term> <note>original: "Zahl"; in Böhme’s thought, "number" often refers to the inherent measure, limit, or divine blueprint of a created being.</note> contained within the thing from which it grew. But what is in One Number is <term>incorruptible</term> <note>original: "unzerbrechlich"; literally "unbreakable." Böhme uses this to describe a state of being that is whole and cannot decay because it lacks internal conflict.</note>, for it is only One and nothing more. There is nothing in it that could break it, for no thing that is only One is hostile toward itself. 

However, when two things are in One, there is already <term>opposition</term> <note>original: "Widerwärtigkeit"; refers to the tension or contrariety that arises when two different qualities or "wills" exist within the same space.</note> and strife; for One does not strive against itself, but rather draws into itself and out of itself, and remains One. And even if it seeks more within itself, it finds no more, and thus can never become at odds with itself; for it is one thing—wherever it goes, it goes in one will. For when there are two wills, there is separation, for one wants to op-

Thus the thing rises up from two into one, and each has its own <term>will</term> <note>original: Wille</note>. And if it is now in one body, it is at odds with itself, for it has many wills and requires a <term>judge</term> <note>original: Richter</note> who might separate them and keep the wills in check. But if the wills become strong and will not let themselves be tamed by the judge, then out of one governance two are formed, and there is a conflict where one desires to suppress the other and to elevate itself alone in one being. And if it cannot suppress it, each grows within itself until it reaches its highest <term>number</term> <note>original: Zahl; the pre-ordained limit or divine blueprint of a thing's development.</note>. And when it can grow no more and sees the end of the number, it seeks to break the goal. And in that very will which seeks to break the goal, the <term>prophet</term> <note>original: Prophet</note> is born, who is the mouth of the <term>kingdom</term> <note>original: Reich</note>, and points out the opposition—how the kingdom had grown in one will, but had gone out from itself into many wills.

And so it is then, that the Father, as the first unique will, [has] his evil and disobe[dient]—

*) These beautiful and sublime ideas concerning the origin of nature and the human soul from God—of their connection, their equality with Him, and their difference from Him—seem to have no connection to the little word **Vom** <note>The German preposition "from" in the phrase "Deliver us from evil."</note> to which Jacob Böhme attaches them. For "E" and "vil" <note>original: *Ue* and *bel*; Böhme provides a mystical deconstruction of the German word for "evil" (*Uebel*), treating the letters as symbolic keys to spiritual truths.</note> are two wills within one being: "E" <note>original: *ü*</note> is the fire-child, and "vil" <note>original: *bel*</note> also has two principles. This does not mean being immediately separated (for God’s Name consists of them dwelling within one another), but rather the Angel's will desires to be free from falsehood; it wishes to rule over the evil. It desires to be within God's will and to withstand the evil—the one according to the spirit of this world for the sake of God's wonders, and the other according to the source of wrath for the sake of the wonders of God's anger. For both mothers <note>The "mothers" are the two primary sources: the dark world of wrath and the light world of love.</note> are active and wish to recognize their wonders. Oh, how great are the wonders! Whoever rightly recognizes this has great joy in it! Nothing can be named that is equal to these mysteries. No tongue can express this, for what is better than to have God as a spouse, in God

<meta>This page transitions from the soul's prayer for divine restoration to a mystical-linguistic analysis of how human speech reflects the internal struggle between "wrath" and "love." The "Nature-language" mentioned here is Böhme's core concept that the physical sounds of words reveal their spiritual origin.</meta>

### 56. The Word: Speech, explained according to the Nature-language.

The word: **Speech** <note>original: *Sprach*; Böhme is breaking down the phonetic components of this specific German word to show how the "Nature-language" (*Natursprache*) functions.</note>, forms itself between the teeth, for they bite together, and the *spirit* <note>original: *Geist*; in this context, it refers to both the physical breath and the animating spiritual force behind speech.</note> hisses out through the teeth, and the tongue bends in the middle, and at the front it sharpens itself, as if it were listening to what hissed there and were afraid. But when the spirit grasps the word, it closes the mouth and holds it at the back of the palate above the tongue in the cavity, in the bitter and harsh **quality** <note>Böhme identifies seven fundamental "qualities" or "source-spirits" that make up all reality; "bitter" and "harsh" are the first, more restrictive forces.</note>. There the tongue is startled and nestles into the lower palate. Then the spirit proceeds from the heart and encloses the word—which is held at the back of the palate in the harsh and bitter quality within **wrath**—and it comes forth through the *fierceness* <note>original: *Grimmigkeit*; a technical term in Böhme’s writing for the severe, "dark-fire" side of nature that must be overcome by love.</note> strong and mighty like a king or a prince, and it also opens the mouth and rules with a strong spirit from the heart through the entire mouth and outside of the mouth, and makes a powerful long syllable, like a spirit that has broken the wrath. Against this, the wrath—with its *cooing* <note>original: *Kirren*; this refers to the vibrating or grating "r" sound in "Sp**r**ach," which Böhme hears as the rumbling of the internal fire.</note> in the harsh and bitter quality, in the back of the palate in the cavity upon the tongue—braces itself and keeps its right for itself, and remains seated in its place, and lets the gentle spirit from the heart pass through it, and thunders with its cooing afterwards, and helps the word...

<term>Nature-language</term> (Natursprache): The mystical idea that true language is not arbitrary, but that the sounds of words directly correspond to the inner essence and divine qualities of the objects they describe.
<term>Spirit</term> (Geist): The active, breathing power that carries the "will" out from the heart into the world.
<term>Tongue</term> (Zunge): Regarded here as a sensory organ that "listens" to the internal spiritual vibrations before shaping them into sound.
<term>Palate</term> (Gaumen): The "cavity" where the restrictive, harsh qualities of sound (and spirit) are concentrated.
<term>Quality</term> (Qualität): The fundamental characteristics or forces (such as bitterness, heat, or sweetness) that constitute all created things.
<term>Wrath</term> (Zorn): The fire-like, egoistic, and harsh force within the soul and nature that requires "breaking" or transformation by love.
<term>Fierceness</term> (Grimmigkeit): The severe and terrifying aspect of the divine fire when it is not tempered by the light of love.
<term>Heart</term> (Herz): The center of the human being from which the "gentle spirit" of love and the true essence of the soul emerge.
<term>Cooing</term> (Kirren): A term for the rough, vibrating sound of the letter "R," which Böhme associates with the mechanical or "wrathful" aspect of nature.

<summary>Böhme provides a mystical anatomy of the word "Speech," describing how the physical mechanics of the mouth and breath mirror the spiritual conflict between harsh wrath and the gentle spirit of the heart.</summary>
<keywords>Nature-language, phonetics, spiritual anatomy, wrath vs. love, Jakob Böhme, linguistics, mysticism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Jakob Böhme’s mystical exploration of the German word "Sprach" (Speech). ...of this world stands, [it] drove. For it is written: **And it was dark upon the deep, and the Spirit of God hovered over the water.** <note>Genesis 1:2. Böhme interprets this foundational biblical verse through his own unique system of mystical physics.</note> The **deep** signifies the innermost **birth** <term>Birth (Geburt): In Böhme’s writing, this refers to a stage, quality, or "working" of the divine nature as it unfolds into existence.</term>, and the **darkness** signifies the outermost, corrupted birth, in which the **wrath** burned, and the **water** signifies the **softening** <note>original: *Sänftigung*; implies a soothing or making gentle of the harsh, "wrathful" forces.</note> of the Spirit.

But that the Spirit hisses through the teeth signifies that the Spirit drove out from the **heart of God** through the **wrath**; but that the teeth remain closed while the Spirit hisses and do not open, that signifies that the **wrath** has not **understood** <note>original: *begriffen*; Böhme is using a double meaning here: the wrath neither physically "grasped/trapped" nor intellectually "comprehended" the Holy Spirit.</note> the Holy Spirit. But that the tongue nestles in the lower palate and sharpens at the front, and does not allow itself to be used for the hissing, that signifies that the outermost birth, together with all creatures that are within it, cannot understand the Holy Spirit who goes out from the innermost birth from the heart of God, nor can they stop or hinder Him with their power. For He goes through all locked chambers and **births**, and needs no unlocking, just as the teeth cannot hold Him back from going out through them.

But that the lips stand open when He hisses through the teeth signifies that with His going forth from the heart of God in the **creation of this world**, He has opened the **gates of heaven** again, and has gone through the gates of **God’s wrath**, and has [overcome] the **wrath** of God...

But the fact that the tongue presses so firmly against the lower palate when the word is uttered signifies the <term>animal spirit (animalischer Geist)<note>In the context of 17th-century mysticism, the "animal spirit" refers to the vital life force or "soul of the breath" that animates the physical body, shared by humans and animals alike.</note></term> of all creatures, and especially of man. The tongue, however, signifies the soul; it is born from the seven spirits of nature and is their son. Whenever the seven spirits desire, the tongue must move according to their pleasure and must demand their cause.

If the sidereal spirits do not become false and dally with the devil, they hide the animal spirit and keep it held fast in their bonds as a treasure when they fight against the devil; just as they hide the tongue when they wrestle with the harsh and bitter <term>quality (Qualität)<note>Böhme uses "quality" (from the Latin *qualitas*) to describe the essential characteristics or "energies" of the divine and natural forces.</note></term> as their most precious jewel. Thus you have a brief and yet truthful guide to the Word which God has spoken, rightly described in the knowledge of the Spirit, according to my—

Now the question arises: what did God say? He said: *Let there be light, and there was light.* The light proceeded from the innermost <term>birth (Geburt)<note>In Böhme’s mystical system, a "birth" is not a single event but a process of unfolding or a specific level of reality being manifested from the divine source.</note></term> and ignited the outermost one once again. But this is not to be understood as meaning that the Godhead is therefore separated from nature; no, it is like body and soul. Nature is the body; the Heart of God is the soul.

Now one might ask: what kind of light was it that was kindled? Was it the sun, or was it the stars? No, the sun and the stars were only created on the fourth day out of that very light.

### 57. Explanation of the Trinity of God.

If one wishes to see God the Son, one must again look at natural things; otherwise, one cannot write of Him. The spirit indeed sees Him, but one cannot express it, for the divine essence consists in a power that cannot be written down. Therefore, we must make use of <term>parables (Gleichnisse)<note>Likenesses or analogies from the physical world used to explain spiritual truths.</note></term> when we wish to speak of God, for in this world we live in <term>fragments (Stückwerk)<note>A reference to 1 Corinthians 13:9: "For we know in part [in Stückwerk] and we prophesy in part." Böhme uses this to emphasize that human knowledge is currently incomplete and piecemeal.</note></term> and have been made out of fragments. For this reason, I would have the reader summoned into that [other] life; there I will speak more precisely and clearly with him about this high <term>article (Artikel)<note>A formal point of doctrine or faith.</note></term>. In the meantime, let the loving reader look to the meaning of the Spirit, and it will not fail: he too will receive a small spark of power from it, if only he hungers for it. Now take note: the <term>Turks and heathens<note>In the 17th century, "Turks" was a common synecdoche for Muslims. Böhme is addressing the theological challenges to the Christian doctrine of the Trinity from non-Christian perspectives.</note></term> say that God has no Son! Here, open your eyes

# Beginning of the creation of the angels before the time of the world.

Now observe: the sun moves in the midst of the <term>deep<note>In Böhme’s cosmology, "the deep" (die Tiefe) refers to the boundless spiritual and physical space of the universe that acts as a medium for divine power.</note></term> between the stars, and gives light and power to all the stars, and tempers the power of all the stars so that everything becomes lovely and full of joy. ### 59. Illness, Medicine, and the Physician.

Since <term>Divine Providence<note>German: *göttliche Vorsehung*. Referring to God's foreknowledge and purposeful guidance of the universe.</note> recognized that Adam would not endure, He created all kinds of medicine for the future illnesses of mankind. For when a clever physician investigates the cause of a disease and gives the patient the appropriate remedy, the hidden power of that remedy begins to work. For when the <term>medication<note>German: *Medicament*.</note> is placed into warm moisture, the life within it begins to rise; it wishes to lift itself up and ignite a light, yet it cannot do so because of the <term>wrath<note>German: *Grimm*. A central term in Böhme’s philosophy representing the harsh, bitter, or "fiery" quality of nature that exists in tension with "light" or "love."</note> that opposes it in its <term>sidereal birth<note>German: *siderische Geburt*. This refers to the physical and astral nature of a thing as it is shaped by the stars (the "sidereal" realm) during its formation in the material world.</note>. However, it is capable of this much: it takes the illness away from the person. For the sidereal life rises up through death and takes away the power of death's sting; and when it triumphs, the person becomes healthy again.

In whatever degree of fire or cold <term>Mercury<note>German: *Mercurius*. In Böhme’s system, Mercury represents the vital, moving spirit or the "workman" within matter that drives all processes.</note> is ignited, and whichever salt among the seven <term>salts<note>German: *Salzen*. Böhme often speaks of seven primary qualities or "forms" of nature, here associated with the alchemical principle of Salt, which represents physical substance and stability.</note> is ignited, such a salt belongs in the <term>cure<note>German: *Cur*. The specific treatment or remedy required for healing.</note>. For illness is a hunger that desires its own <term>likeness<note>German: *Gleichheit*. This is the core principle of Böhme’s medicine: a disease is an "ignited" or over-active quality that can only be pacified by applying a remedy of the same nature.</note>. Now the quality of life—which in the beginning of its <term>original state<note>German: *Urstand*. The primal, pure condition of a thing before it fell into discord.</note> stood in joy—is the root; and illness is its excessive ignition, by which its order is torn apart. But the root, in its hunger, desires the likeness that was taken from it through that ignition. At this point, the ignition is stronger than the root, and therefore one must satisfy the hunger of the ignition and give to it what it is itself. For every taste desires only its own kind, and once its hunger is satisfied, it rejoices within itself. From this, the illness sinks into a state of rest within itself, for the hunger of opposition ceases to <term>operate<note>German: *qualificiren*. A technical term in Böhme’s philosophy meaning to interact, exert influence, or manifest a specific quality.</note>.

Therefore, it is necessary for the <term>physician<note>German: *Medicus*.</note> to know how to handle <term>medicines<note>German: *Medicamenten*.</note> according to their likeness, so that he does not provoke them further and lead them into a different quality. But nothing is better than for every thing to be left in its innate <term>virtue<note>German: *Tugend*. In the historical sense, "virtue" refers to the inherent power or medicinal efficacy of a substance.</note>, provided that its <term>wrath<note>German: *Grimm*. This represents the harsh, bitter, or "fiery" aspect of nature that causes conflict and disease when out of balance.</note> is transformed into its own joy, so that its own virtue—according to its good part—is raised up and brought into governance. Thus, through likeness, it is powerful in all illnesses...

<summary>Böhme explains that illness is an imbalance or "hunger" caused by an over-ignited quality of nature, which the physician must cure by providing a remedy of the same nature to transform "wrath" back into "joy."</summary>
<keywords>Mercury, Salt, cure, illness as hunger, likeness, original state, physician, medicines, virtue, wrath, transformation</keywords>

--- Page 220 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "hunger" of natural qualities, explaining that healing occurs when a specific essence is satisfied by its own likeness, allowing the inner life-light to burn clearly again without external interference.</meta>

...enough without any other interference. For the origin in life desires no multiplicity, but only its own <term>likeness<note>German: *Gleichheit*. A central concept in Böhme’s thought, suggesting that things are only at peace when paired with their own corresponding nature.</note>, so that it may stand, live, and burn in its own power.

All sharpness of taste is <term>Salt<note>German: *Salz*. In alchemical philosophy, Salt represents the physical body and the principle of stability or "fixation."</note>, whatever it may be in this world, nothing excepted; and all smell proceeds from <term>Sulfur<note>German: *Schwefel*. Sulfur represents the soul, the combustible principle, and the source of inner light and growth.</note>, and in all movement <term>Mercury<note>German: *Mercurius*. The "workman" or "vital spirit" that mediates between Salt and Sulfur.</note> is the distinction in both smell, power, and taste. By my use of the term Mercury, however, I mean the <term>wheel of the birth<note>German: *Rad der Geburt*. A recurring image in Böhme’s work describing the dynamic, revolving process by which nature and spirit manifest.</note> of all being—not a dead thing, but a living one, as the strongest force according to the property of the "dry poison." Therefore, it is necessary for the artist and <term>physician<note>original Latin: *Medicus*.</note> to know such things; otherwise, he cannot cure any illness, unless he happens upon a cure by chance. 

For if he can obtain the Salt according to the property of its hunger, with such an <term>Oil<note>German: *Oel*. Böhme uses oil as a metaphor for the spiritual lubricant or fuel that allows the light of life to burn without the "friction" of harsh qualities.</note> as it desires, then the illness is soon gone; for he restores the Oil to the property of the love of the light, from which life begins to shine brightly again. For every illness in the body is nothing other than a corruption of the Oil from which the light of life burns and shines. For when the light of life shines and burns brightly in the Oil, it drives out all poisonous influences and effects, just as the day drives out the night.

<meta>This page transitions from the technical alchemical descriptions of "Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury" to a personal, autobiographical account of Jacob Böhme’s own spiritual awakening. ### 60. Origin and Spread of the Writings of Jacob Böhme.

When, before my <term>insight<note>German: *Erkenntniß*. For Böhme, this is not just intellectual knowledge, but a direct, mystical realization or "recognition" of truth.</note> and revelation from God, I looked upon the great depth of this world—including the sun and stars, as well as the clouds, rain, and snow—and contemplated in my spirit the entire creation of this world; and [pondering] what a tiny little spark man might be esteemed before God compared to the great works of heaven and earth; and finding that it goes as well for the godless as for the pious, and that barbarian nations possessed the best lands, I became entirely <term>melancholy<note>German: *melancholisch*. In the 17th century, melancholy was considered a profound spiritual and physical heaviness that often preceded a religious "breakthrough."</note> and very distressed, and no Scripture could comfort me, even though I was quite well-acquainted with it. 

When, in such <term>tribulation<note>German: *Trübsal*. This refers to a "dark night of the soul" or a period of intense spiritual suffering and doubt.</note>, my spirit earnestly lifted itself up into God, and my whole heart and <term>disposition<note>German: *Gemüth*. A key term in Böhme’s thought, referring to the seat of the soul’s desires, emotions, and will—the "inner weather" of the person.</note>, along with all my thoughts and will, enclosed themselves therein—wrestling without ceasing with the love and mercy of God, and refusing to let go unless He should bless and enlighten me and take my sadness from me—then the spirit broke through. 

And as I continued in my struggle and exerted all my strength, my spirit broke through into the innermost part of the <term>Godhead<note>German: *Gottheit*. The absolute, unmanifested essence of the Divine.</note>, and was received there with love, just as a bridegroom embraces his dear bride. This triumph of my spirit can...

...[and it can] only be compared with the resurrection from the dead, where life is born in the midst of death.

In this light, not only has my <term>insight<note>German: *Erkenntniß*. Within myself, I saw it clearly, lying like a <term>chaos<note>German: *Chaos*. In Böhme's philosophy, this refers to the unformed, primal potential of the divine nature before it manifests into order.</note> in a great depth; but its unfolding was impossible for me. It opened itself within me only from time to time, like a growing plant, and it took twelve years before I could bring it into the external world. It was with me as with a young tree that endures many a wind, frost, and snow, yet it grows, blooms, and bears fruit.

For my outward occupation was a common trade <note>Böhme was a master shoemaker in Görlitz; he emphasizes that his spiritual wisdom did not come from academic training.</note> by which I supported myself honestly for a long time; meanwhile, however, my inward occupation was earnestly directed toward how I might die away from my <term>selfhood<note>German: *Ichheit*. The individual ego or the self-centered nature that Böhme believes must be surrendered to God.</note> and my <term>self-will<note>German: *Selbstwollen*. The human drive to act independently of the divine will.</note> in the death of Christ, and how I might arise in His will as a new, divine spirit and will. After a hard struggle, God crowned me with divine joy, which I do not know how to describe, but which I heartily wish for all children of God. Afterwards <meta>catchword: Afterwards</meta>

...the sun shone for me for a considerable time, yet not continuously; and when it hid itself, I could hardly understand my own work. From this, man should recognize that knowledge is not his own, but belongs to God, and that God knows within the soul of man what and how He wills.

I intended to keep my writings with me for my entire life and to give them to no one; but through the providence of the Most High, it happened that I entrusted a portion of them to a person, through whom they became public without my prior knowledge. Consequently, my first book, the ***Aurora***, was taken from me <note>In 1613, the city council of Görlitz confiscated Böhme's manuscript and ordered him to stop writing after complaints from the local head pastor, Gregorius Richter.</note>. Böhme uses this in the biblical sense (from the Parable of the Talents), referring to a divinely entrusted gift that one is obligated to use for the benefit of others.</note>. <meta>This page continues Böhme’s personal reflection on his divine calling. with an open face; who will take that from me? What then do I fear of the world in a heavenly matter? I know that I serve Jesus Christ, who makes me like His image; if He has died and risen, why then should I not suffer and die with Him and rise in Him?

When I reflect and think why I write in this way, and do not leave it to other ingenious scholars, I find that my spirit is ignited in this <term>Being<note>German: *Wesen*. For Böhme, this refers to the fundamental essence or "thingness" of God and the cosmos.</note></term> of which I write; there is a living, running fire of these things in my spirit. Therefore, whatever else I intend for myself, this thing always wells up from above, and so I am captured by it in my spirit, and it is laid upon me as a work that I must pursue. 

Since it is now my work, which my spirit drives, I will write it for myself as a <term>memorial<note>German: *Memorial*. A personal record or testament intended to preserve his spiritual insights for future reflection.</note></term>, and in exactly such a way as I recognize it in my spirit, and then in the manner in which I came to it; and I will set down nothing foreign that I have not experienced myself, so that I may not be found a liar before God.

<summary>Böhme expresses his unwavering commitment to his spiritual writing, describing it as an internal "fire" and a divinely imposed duty that he must record from his own direct experience.</summary>
<keywords>Jesus Christ, spirit, fire, memorial, God, disgrace, resurrection, personal experience, divine calling</keywords>

--- Page 231 ---
<meta>This page continues Böhme’s personal reflection on his divine calling. He has just described his writing as a "memorial" of his spiritual experiences. Here, he addresses the specific internal and external conflicts he faces—the tension between his worldly duties as a craftsman and the spiritual compulsion to record his visions.</meta>

I know well enough that the <term>children of the flesh<note>A biblical reference to those who live only for worldly and physical concerns, rather than spiritual matters.</note></term> will mock me and say that I should attend to my <note>Böhme was a master shoemaker; he is referring here to his trade and the need to provide for his family.</note>profession, leave these things alone, and instead take better care of what fills the bellies of myself and my kin, leaving philosophizing to those who have studied it and are called to do so. With this <term>temptation<note>German: *Anfechtung*. In the Lutheran tradition, this refers to a deep spiritual trial or a crisis of faith where one feels attacked by doubt or the devil.</note></term>, the devil has already given me many a blow, so that I resolved to cease from this work; but my intention became too heavy for me. For whenever I cared only for earthly matters, the gate of heaven was bolted shut against my <term>insight<note>German: *Erkenntniß*. This implies a direct, experiential spiritual knowledge or "cognition" granted by God, rather than book-learning.</note></term>.

Then my soul grew anxious, as if it were captured by the devil, and the spirit did not let up until it broke through the <term>dead reason<note>German: *todte Vernunft*. Böhme views human logic or "reason" as being "dead" or blind to spiritual truths unless it is illuminated by divine spirit.</note></term> once more, shattered the gates of darkness, and thereby received new life and new strength.

Since I therefore feel that my eternal salvation depends upon it, I will let God rule, take my carnal reason captive, and strive after the impulses and insight of the spirit. And even if my earthly body should fall into want, or even perish entirely, I will no longer inquire after it. I will write according to my vision and look to no man’s authority. You must not understand this as if my <term>old man<note>German: *alter Mensch*. A Pauline theological concept referring to the unregenerate, worldly nature of a human being that must be overcome for spiritual rebirth.</note></term> were a living saint or an—

<summary>Böhme describes the spiritual torment he experiences when trying to prioritize his worldly trade over his divine visions, concluding that he must follow the Spirit's lead regardless of poverty or social mockery.</summary>
<keywords>temptation, reason, soul, shoemaker, spiritual insight, devil, carnal nature, salvation, authority</keywords>

--- Page 232 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's defense against accusations of spiritual pride. Having previously discussed the struggle between worldly duties and his divine calling, he now emphasizes his own insignificance. He completes a word from the previous page—"angel"—denying that he possesses any superhuman status, and instead frames his writing as a direct result of God working through his surrendered "selfhood."</meta>

[an An]gel; no, friend, he sits with all people in the house of wrath and death, and is, like all people, full of infirmity and flaws.

I write, and yet I do not do it; for I, who am the "I" that I am, know nothing and have learned nothing; thus it is not I who does it now, but God does in me what He wills. Therefore, I live not for myself, but for Him, and thus we are but one in Christ, as a tree with many boughs and branches; and He bears fruit in every little twig as He wills, and so I have brought His life into mine, so that I am reconciled with Him in His love. For because Christ is born in me and lives in my <term>nothingness<note>German: *Nichtheit*. This refers to the mystical state of total humility where the human ego is surrendered so that God can act through the individual.</note></term>, He will surely crush the head of the serpent—that is, my <term>selfhood<note>German: *Ichheit*. Also translated as "I-hood" or "egoity," this is Böhme’s term for the selfish, fallen human nature that seeks to be its own master apart from God.</note></term>—and kill the evil man within my selfhood, so that He who He is Himself may live.

Indeed, if I were to write of myself that I were a great master of Scripture or of the arts from the schools of this world, that would not be true; I am a simple, plain man, and I have not gained my <term>insight<note>German: *Erkenntniß*. For Böhme, this is not academic learning but a direct spiritual realization or "gnosis" granted by divine grace.</note></term> and high knowledge through worldly art or <term>reason<note>German: *Vernunft*. Böhme often distinguishes between "blind reason" (human logic) and "divine understanding."</note></term>. Nor have I ever sought great skill, but from my youth onward, I sought nothing else but the salvation of my soul and how I might inherit and possess the kingdom of God. <meta>This page continues the author’s spiritual autobiography and his defense of his writings. He transitions from discussing his personal insignificance to the intense internal struggle required to overcome his fallen nature and receive divine revelation.</meta>

Since I felt a powerful urge against the <term>Divine<note>German: *Göttliche*.</note></term> in my flesh and blood, I set myself so firmly in <term>battle<note>German: *Streit*. This refers to the internal spiritual warfare common in mystical traditions.</note></term> against my own corrupt nature that I intended, through God's assistance, to overcome and break my innate evil will. I sought to hide myself in God's heart from the terrible storm-weather of God's wrath and from the fury of the devil, so that the Spirit of God might rule, drive, and lead me. For I am a child whose understanding hangs on the palate of its mother, and I have no power nor understanding except what the mother gives me. <note>The "Mother" is a recurring metaphor for Divine Wisdom (*Sophia*), who nurtures the soul of the mystic with spiritual truth as a mother feeds an infant.</note> I lie in a faint, like a dying man. Yet the Most High raises me up with His breath, so that I walk according to His wind.

Only from Him do I take my <term>insight<note>German: *Erkenntniß*. As noted previously, this is a direct, experiential "gnosis" rather than intellectual study.</note></term> and from no other thing. I also wish to know nothing else but that same God, who gives my spirit certainty so that I may constantly believe and trust in Him. And even if an angel from heaven told it to me, I still would not be able to believe it, much less grasp it, for I would always doubt whether it was indeed so. But in this way the Sun itself rises in my spirit; therefore I am certain of it, and I see for myself the arrival and birth of the holy angels, and of all things in heaven and in this world. For—

<summary>The author describes his desperate struggle to suppress his "corrupt nature" and surrender to God's will. He claims that his spiritual knowledge is not learned from others, but is a direct, internal revelation from God that provides absolute certainty.</summary>

# Life,
# Fates, Talents, and Character
# of Jacob Böhme.

<note>Jacob Böhme (1575–1624) was a German shoemaker and Christian mystic. He is a foundational figure in modern mysticism, known for his complex visions of the divine nature and the origin of good and evil, which he believed were revealed to him through spiritual "illumination" rather than formal academic study.</note>

--- Page 237 ---
<meta>This page marks the beginning of a biographical sketch of the famous German mystic Jacob Böhme, focusing on his humble origins and early life.</meta>

The poor Jacob Böhme—despised and persecuted during his life, and misunderstood even to this day—was born in 1575 in Altseidenberg, near <term>Görlitz in Upper Lusatia</term> <note>A historical region in what is now eastern Germany, bordering Poland and the Czech Republic</note>. His father, Jacob, and his mother, Ursula, were poor, honest farming folk of that place. They raised him in true fear of God, and in school, in addition to instruction in Christianity, they had him learn a bit of writing. During this time, he herded their cattle until they apprenticed him to the <term>shoemaker trade</term> <note>Despite his profound philosophical influence, Böhme remained a working cobbler for much of his life, which led his detractors to mock him as an uneducated craftsman</note> in Görlitz. Already in his boyhood years, his imagination seems to have been very active, as evidenced by a couple of anecdotes from the story of his youth, where he most likely mistook some extraordinarily vivid dreams for actual facts.

While he was still herding cattle, he once climbed the <term>Landeskrone</term> <note>A prominent volcanic hill near Görlitz, which remains a local landmark</note>—located about an hour's journey from Görlitz—during the noon hour. On this hill, he found a

<summary>This page introduces the early life of the mystic Jacob Böhme, describing his humble upbringing as the son of poor farmers and his apprenticeship as a shoemaker in Görlitz.</summary>
<keywords>Jacob Böhme, biography, Altseidenberg, Görlitz, Upper Lusatia, childhood, shoemaker, Landeskrone, mysticism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the narrative of Jacob Böhme’s youth, specifically focusing on two formative, supernatural anecdotes: the discovery of a hidden treasure on the Landeskrone and the encounter with a mysterious stranger who predicted his future.</meta>

entrance, and as he went in, he beheld a large vat of money. He was so startled by this, however, that he rushed back in great haste without taking any of the coins for himself. When he later climbed the hill several times with other boys, he could no longer find the entrance. Since he told this occurrence to Lord von **Frankenberg** <note>Abraham von Frankenberg (1593–1652) was a Silesian nobleman and mystic who became Böhme’s most important biographer and a devoted student of his work</note> himself and also showed him the place on the **Landeskrone** where the entrance was supposed to have been, the story cannot be a pure invention; nonetheless, the fact that he could never find the entrance again suggests that this event could not have been a physical reality. Rather, it was likely only a vivid dream, which Böhme, through a trick of the mind, mistook for an actual fact—just as there are several examples of people who are unable to distinguish their extraordinarily vivid dreams from reality.

The same can be said of the stranger who bought a pair of shoes from him and prophesied his destiny. But take heart and remain steadfast, for you are dear to God, and He is merciful toward you." Böhme was deeply shaken by this and kept the man’s words and face forever in his mind; from that moment on, he became more solemn and attentive in everything he did.

Finally, he returned from his travels <note>original: "Wanderschaft," the traditional years a journeyman spent traveling to gain experience before becoming a master</note> to Görlitz, and here in 1594 he became both a master and a bridegroom, marrying the virtuous maiden Catharina, the legitimate daughter of the honorable Hans Kunschmann, a citizen and butcher in Görlitz. This Dr. Kober had always been Böhme’s active friend; he had assisted him during his final illness and arranged an honorable burial for him.

<meta>This page continues the biography of the mystic Jacob Böhme, specifically following the account of his third spiritual awakening in 1610. The main text describes the composition and controversial reception of his first book, while the footnote concludes a rationalist critique of Böhme's psychological state.</meta>

...not let the received grace <note>original: "pfangene Gnade"; the first syllable "em-" was at the end of the previous page</note> slip from his memory, he wrote, though only for himself, in the year 1612 his first book, *The Dawn in its Rising* <note>original: "Morgenröthe im Aufgange," usually known by its Latin title, *Aurora*</note>. By chance, an acquaintance from the nobility saw this manuscript lying at his home and, after much pleading, received it from Böhme to read through; however, he hurriedly had it copied day and night. In this way, it soon became known to several others, and finally also came into the hands of the head pastor of Görlitz, Gregorius Richter. One cannot blame the Primarius <note>the chief or senior pastor of a city</note> Richter for withholding his approval of this writing, for there is much of the mystical, the <term>visionary</term> <note>original: "Schwärmerischen"; in this period, "Schwärmerei" referred to religious enthusiasm that the orthodox church viewed as fanatical or dangerously detached from scripture</note>, and the confusing within it. 

But the manner in which he expressed his displeasure about it, and about its author, was both unwise and uncharitable. Through a friendly conversation with Böhme, he likely could have given Böhme's spirit a different direction; but instead, he scolded and condemned him and his writing most violently from the pulpit, until finally the City Council of Görlitz summoned Jacob Böhme before them on July 26, 1613, took the manuscript from him, and kept it in the City Hall—

---

<meta>This page continues the account of the conflict between the mystic Jacob Böhme and the ecclesiastical authorities in Görlitz. It describes the period of silence imposed upon Böhme following the seizure of his first manuscript by the City Council.</meta>

[The Council] guarded [the manuscript], and forbade Böhme from writing any more books, while forbidding the Primarius \*) <note>The "Primarius" was the chief or senior pastor of the city, in this case, Gregorius Richter.</note> from further slandering Böhme from the pulpit.

Böhme obeyed his authorities <note>original: "Obrigkeit"</note> and wrote nothing more for the following seven years. Because of this, he felt great anxiety and restlessness; from the outside, he had to endure much mockery and contempt, and from within, his inner light was withdrawn from him through a lack of exercise of his cognitive powers. Yet, a fierce longing for new and ever higher knowledge remained within him. Thus, a painful struggle arose in his mind between obedience to the authorities and the impulse to write, until finally, through some external encouragement, he emerged victorious. He had considered his *The Dawn in its Rising* <note>original: "Morgenröthe im Aufgange," Böhme's first work, often referred to by its Latin title, *Aurora*</note> to be destroyed and had not seen it for three years. Unexpectedly, a copy of it was sent to him by learned men, along with an exhortation to reveal his talent. The Primarius had also failed to keep his word and had preached against him once more. At the same time, as he expresses it in his twelfth letter, there came to him—

---

\*) Richter was a preacher in Görlitz from 1590 to 1624; during this period, in which the plague <note>original: "Pest"</note> raged in Görlitz three times, he wrote nearly 6,000 sermons.

<meta>This page continues the biography of the mystic Jacob Böhme (1575–1624). It describes the end of his seven-year silence, his decision to abandon his trade as a shoemaker to focus on his spiritual writings, and the mixture of support from the nobility and mockery from the common populace that he experienced.</meta>

the Most High came to his aid again with His breath, and awakened the divine light within him for the fourth time. These circumstances combined finally moved him to begin writing anew in 1619; he gave up his trade <note>original: "Handwerk"; Böhme was a master shoemaker by trade</note> due to a lack of business <note>original: "Verlag"; here referring to the commercial outlet or capital for his shoemaking wares</note> and continued to write until his end, producing a total of 31 works.

He already had many friends and admirers of his writings in Silesia and Lusatia, most of whom were noblemen or physicians. From these people, he received his meager livelihood, as they sent him some grain and money, partly as gifts and partly as payment <note>original: "Honorar"</note> for transcribing his writings. Böhme also occasionally traveled to visit his friends himself, and was then likely sent away with some gifts in return for his efforts and the verbal explanations he provided to them regarding his works. Although he now associated with nothing but educated and respected men, he was nevertheless mocked and ridiculed by the frivolous and unkind from all walks of life. For instance, a physician once gave a young man—who was to accompany Böhme to the Seifersdorf estate of the nobleman Lord von Schweinich—an *Ortsthaler* <note>a silver coin worth a quarter of a Thaler</note>, on the condition that he push Böhme into a puddle along the way. Unfortunately for Böhme, Lord von Schweinich’s brother-in-law, along with his wife and children, happened to be visiting at that very time. This man was hostile toward Böhme and pressured him to prophesy something for him. Böhme then confronted him with his ungodly life and told him what would happen to him if he did not mend his ways. The nobleman, shamed by this, became violently enraged against Böhme and even wanted to physically assault him with blows.

But Lord von Schweinich secured peace for Böhme by having him taken, along with six dishes of food, to the local pastor, with the request that he keep him for the night until he could have him brought back to Görlitz <note>The city in Upper Lusatia where Böhme spent most of his life</note> the following morning. The cause of the contempt to which Böhme was so often subjected lay by no means solely in his religious enthusiasm <term>Schwärmerei: a derogatory term used by Böhme’s contemporaries to dismiss his mystical visions as irrational or fanatical</term>, but primarily in his unimposing physical appearance, in his simple demeanor, in his trade <note>Böhme was a master shoemaker</note>, and likely also in his poor clothing; all of which, taken together, created a great contrast with a man who, in a certain respect

<meta>This page continues the physical description of the mystic Jacob Böhme and transitions into the final conflict of his life involving his antagonist, the Senior Pastor Gregor Richter.</meta>

...[a contrast to that which] as a writer, seemed to elevate him above all philosophers and religious teachers. Abraham von Frankenberg <note>Abraham von Frankenberg (1593–1652), a nobleman and mystic who became Böhme's friend and first biographer</note>, one of Böhme's greatest admirers, describes his outward appearance as follows: "Jacob Böhme’s external physical form was worn down and of poor appearance; he was of small stature, with a low forehead, prominent temples, a slightly hooked nose, eyes that were gray and shone almost sky-blue, a short, thin beard, and a low-pitched voice, yet possessed of gracious speech, modest in gestures, humble in words, lowly in conduct, patient in suffering, and gentle of heart."

From 1619 to 1623, Böhme had once again written a great number of works and distributed them in manuscript copies among his friends without being called to account by the Council of Görlitz <note>The city council had previously forbidden Böhme from writing after his first work, *Aurora*, caused a religious scandal</note>. The Senior Pastor <term>Primarius: the head minister of the city's main church</term> Richter continued, it is true, to rail against him, but he did not yet dare to undertake any more active measures against him. 

However, at the beginning of the year 1624—which was to be the final year of life for both Richter and Böhme—Richter was so provoked by a visit he received from Böhme and by Böhme’s first work to appear in print, *Of True Repentance* and *Of True Resignation* <note>original: "von wahrer Buße und wahrer Gelassenheit"; these were parts of "The Way to Christ," the only book of Böhme's published during his lifetime with his consent</note>, that he employed every means in his power to suppress him and his writings.

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the growing tension between Jacob Böhme and the Senior Pastor Gregor Richter in Görlitz. It details a specific financial dispute involving Böhme's brother-in-law that served as the catalyst for Richter's final, aggressive campaign against the mystic.</meta>

A young baker, Böhme's brother-in-law, had borrowed some money from the Senior Pastor <term>Primarius: the head minister of the city's main church</term> Richter around Christmas 1623 to bake Stollen <note>A traditional German fruit bread usually eaten during the Christmas season</note>. However, he paid the money back after eight weeks without interest, in the hope that the Senior Pastor would not charge anything for such a short time, but would instead be satisfied with the large Christmas cake <note>original: "Striezel," a long, braided yeast bread</note> that he had presented to him as a gift during the Christmas festival. But the preacher Richter lashed out at him and threatened him with God's wrath and curse. The simple baker was so terrified by this that he fell into a deep melancholy <note>original: "Schwermuth," a term used in the 17th century to describe a state of profound spiritual depression or despair</note> and doubted his own salvation, because he had so angered the preacher and heard such a curse from him. He wandered about sighing for several days, spoke to himself in great distress, and gave no answer to anyone. 

Finally, in this distress, his wife took refuge with her cousin, Jacob Böhme. When Böhme, after many comforting and friendly words of encouragement, learned the cause of the man's melancholy, he went straight to Richter. He presented the incident to him with modesty, asked him to forgive the young man, and offered to gladly pay the interest himself, asking Richter only to state how much he desired. But Richter lashed out at him and said: "As for this rag-stitcher <note>original: "Zerrfleck," a derogatory nickname Richter used for Böhme, mockingly referring to his trade as a cobbler and suggesting he was a lowly patcher of scraps</note>, he should be gone and mind his own business." Böhme, however, asked him once more for forgiveness and for a determination of the interest <note>original: "Interessen," meaning the financial interest due on the loan</note>. ...chamber door. Böhme went, and said as he was leaving: "God keep your Reverence." The Magistrate <term>the city council or governing body</term> did not know how to decide the matter fairly, and because they feared the vehemence of the preacher from the pulpit <note>meaning the influential Head Pastor Richter</note>, they decided to banish the innocent Böhme from the city. But when this request was refused and he was told that the judgment of the Magistrate could not be changed—having already heard that he was to be escorted immediately from the Town Hall out of the city with insult and mockery—he replied: "Yes, dear gentlemen, let it be so, since it cannot be otherwise; I am content." Thus he was banished from the city, and he remained away through the night.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the conflict between the mystic Jacob Böhme and the Görlitz head pastor, Gregor Richter. It specifically addresses the "lampoon" (Pasquill) written by Richter in Latin in March 1624, which Böhme views not merely as a personal insult, but as a spiritual manifestation of evil.</meta>

...[slanderous] lampoon <note>original: "Pasquill"; a biting, often scurrilous satirical attack.</note> in the Latin language, written on a single sheet of paper; in it, Satan has exposed the Pharisaic heart <note>Böhme uses "Pharisaic" to describe the religious establishment—specifically Richter—who he believed prioritized outward dogma and status over internal spiritual truth.</note>, which has occurred by God’s permission so that the people may come to know and flee from the poison of this Pharisaic heart. And I almost believe that this lampoon was dictated by the greatest of devils, for he has revealed his claws so openly in it that one clearly sees he is a liar and a murderer; it will serve as a warning to many people to take better care of their souls. For indeed, among us, this lampoon is attributed to Satan by almost all scholars.

You may also clearly and reasonably recognize for yourselves that the hand of the Lord has, by His will, led me until now and brought me to such knowledge <note>original: "Erkenntniß"; Böhme uses this term to denote direct spiritual insight or revelation rather than academic learning.</note>, with which I have in turn willingly served many people. This is because I did not receive my learning <note>original: "Wissenschaft"; in the 17th century, this referred to a systematic body of knowledge or wisdom.</note> from schooling or books, but from the great Book of All Beings <note>A central concept in Böhme’s thought; he believed that all of creation is a "book" or "signature" that can be read by a soul illuminated by God.</note>, which the Lord’s hand has opened within me. But in that same book, a cross—as the sign of victory of Jesus Christ—is the true understanding. Before this sign of victory, the devil is so terrified that he might burst with rage; for that reason, he has stirred up great storm-winds from his Sea of Death against me and has shot his cruel jets of water at me, intending to drown me.
