--- Page 256 ---
<meta>This page continues the account of Jacob Böhme’s conflict with the orthodox clergy, specifically Gregor Richter. Böhme describes the spiritual protection he feels against Richter’s attacks and reflects on how the persecution has actually helped spread his writings, particularly his work on "True Resignation."</meta>

Rays [of malice] have until now all missed their mark; for the cross with the victory banner of Jesus Christ has shielded me, and struck the poisonous rays to the ground.

The cause of such anger has been the published, printed little book, *On Repentance and True Resignation* <note>original: "Buß und wahrer Gelassenheit"; *Gelassenheit* is a central term in German mysticism, describing a state of yielding one's self-will to God's will.</note>, which has brought much benefit. This has annoyed the Pharisaic spirit <note>Böhme frequently uses "Pharisaic" to describe the established church leadership who, in his view, prioritized external dogma over inner spiritual transformation.</note>, that such a fundamental truth should be revealed; he fears that such a teaching and life would also be demanded of him—which is not to his taste, because he sits in the lust of the flesh and serves the idol Bacchus <note>original: "Bacho"; the Roman god of wine, used here to accuse his opponent of worldly indulgence and drunkenness.</note> in carnal desire. 

But know this for your information: his slanders and lies have only served to publicize and reveal my little book, so that now almost everyone—nobility and scholars, as well as simple folk—desire to read it and hold it very dear; which book in a short time has become known throughout almost all of Europe and is very beloved everywhere, including at the Electoral Court of Saxony. I have been invited there for a conversation with high-ranking people, which I have agreed to carry out at the conclusion of the Leipzig Fair <note>The Leipziger Messe was one of the most important trade fairs in Europe, serving as a major hub for the distribution of books and ideas.</note>. Who knows what might happen there, whether the mouth of the impudent slanderer might be stopped and the truth might be planted. 

To Christian Bernhard, a tax collector in Sagan <note>A town in Silesia, now Żagań in modern-day Poland.</note>, he wrote among other things on April 4: "I would like to know what people in your area say about..."

<summary>Jacob Böhme reflects on how the persecution by the religious establishment backfired, causing his writings on spiritual resignation to gain popularity among the nobility and scholars across Europe. He notes an invitation to the Electoral Court of Saxony to discuss his teachings.</summary>
<keywords>Jacob Böhme, Gelassenheit, True Resignation, religious persecution, Bacchus, Leipzig Fair, Saxony, Christian Bernhard, Sagan</keywords>

--- Page 257 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of Jacob Böhme’s persecution by the ecclesiastical authorities in Görlitz. The previous page ended with Böhme discussing how the slander against his book "On Repentance" actually increased its popularity across Europe and led to an invitation to the Electoral Court of Saxony.</meta>

...concerns the printed booklet, even if it is slandered? Among us, many hungry hearts rejoice over it. But it is not to the taste of the chief evil man <note>Böhme refers here to his primary antagonist, the Lutheran Superintendent Gregor Richter.</note>, because it teaches repentance and prayer.”

Böhme now learned that he was to be summoned before the Council <term>Rath: the city council of Görlitz, which held both administrative and judicial power</term> again to give an account of himself. He therefore drafted his defense in writing, which he intended to present to the Council; however, at Richter’s instigation, the Council did not accept it. Böhme gave Johann Sigmund von Schweinich <note>A Silesian nobleman and early follower of Böhme who provided him with both protection and financial support.</note>, in a letter dated April 6, the following report regarding this incident: 

“Now I let you know that Satan is so greatly enraged with us, as if we had destroyed all of hell for him, though it has only begun in a few individuals to storm his robber’s castle. But because we no longer wish to grant him lodging within ourselves, he has become so furious with us that he intends to wipe us from this world, so that no more such little herbs may grow in his presumed garden, which he planted with sin in Adam. 

Yet we wish to leave it to our God, for what purpose He intends to use us as His newly planted little roses, and with patience and hope to stand still beneath the cross of our Lord Jesus Christ, and listen to what the Lord will say, and what He wishes to do with His instrument.”

<summary>Jacob Böhme describes the Council's refusal to hear his written defense due to Pastor Richter's interference and expresses his spiritual resolve through a letter to his patron, Schweinich.</summary>
<keywords>Jacob Böhme, Gregor Richter, City Council, Görlitz, Johann Sigmund von Schweinich, spiritual warfare, persecution, religious defense</keywords>

--- Page 258 ---
<meta>This page continues the account of Jacob Böhme’s return to Görlitz and the intensified campaign of character assassination led by his primary adversary, the Head Pastor Gregor Richter. Böhme describes how Richter used both his pulpit and his influence in neighboring regions to turn the civil authorities against him.</meta>

When I returned home from your company, I found that Satan had prepared a bed for me, in which he would have liked to lay me, had God not prevented it through several pious hearts to whom our path is better known. For the chief Pharisee, acting as the **Head Pastor** <term>Primarius: the title given to the lead pastor or superintendent of the city’s churches; here referring to Böhme's rival, Gregor Richter</term>, has raged so violently against the printed booklet as if it had murdered his son and seized all his property. He has poured out a heap of lies against me, along with frivolous slanders, as can be seen in his public libelous pamphlet <term>Pasquill: a satirical or defamatory writing meant to publicly disgrace someone</term> which is attached here.

He has not only spread such shameful lies and insults from the pulpit, but has also written to Liegnitz <note>original: "Lignitz", modern-day Legnica in Poland</note> to Pastor Frisius. He requested that Frisius not only rail against the matter from his own pulpit—as he indeed did—but also have it printed. He further urged him to complain about me to the Noble Council in Görlitz, presenting it in the name of all the priests within the Liegnitz jurisdiction <term>Weichbild: a medieval legal term for the territory within a city's jurisdiction</term>, as if this complaint against my writings—and especially the printed booklet *The Way to Christ*—had originated from all of them. Frisius did exactly this, and at our Head Pastor's request, he reported me to the Noble Council of Görlitz with a deceitful letter.

Beyond this, our Head Pastor has frequently run to the most prominent gentlemen of our city and has so violently [spread] lies about me—

<summary>Jacob Böhme recounts the aggressive efforts of Head Pastor Gregor Richter to suppress his work "The Way to Christ" through pulpit denunciations and political maneuvering between Görlitz and Liegnitz.</summary>
<keywords>Gregor Richter, Pastor Frisius, Liegnitz, Görlitz Council, The Way to Christ, religious persecution, libel, Primarius</keywords>

--- Page 259 ---
<meta>This page continues Jacob Böhme’s account of the legal and social pressure exerted by the Head Pastor Gregor Richter upon his return to Görlitz. Böhme describes the Council's reaction to his writings and the conflicting opinions among the city's leadership regarding his potential banishment.</meta>

...raged and accused me, and demanded that as soon as I arrived home, I should be thrown into prison and banished from the city. He also filed such a deceitful letter of complaint with the Council, heating up hell for me and "preparing a bath" <note>An idiom meaning to prepare a harsh or unpleasant reception, or to make things very difficult for someone</note> for me.

However, since most of the gentlemen of the Council had already read my printed booklet <note>Referring to the collection of Böhme's devotional treatises titled *The Way to Christ*, published in 1624</note> and found nothing unchristian in it—indeed, it had become well-liked by several of them, as well as by many citizens—some considered the Head Pastor’s <term>Primarius: the title of the lead pastor or superintendent of the city's churches, here referring to Böhme’s chief opponent, Gregor Richter</term> demand to be unjust. They spoke against it and said there was no lawful cause for such persecution against me; they argued this religion was nothing new, but was the very foundation of the ancient holy fathers, in whose works one could find more of the same.

Some, however, especially those whom the Head Pastor had won over, thought it best to summon me before the Noble Council and threaten me, saying I should take care that neither the Emperor nor the **Elector** <term>Churfürst: one of the elite German princes who selected the Holy Roman Emperor; in this case, the Elector of Saxony</term> were stirred up by the priests to seize me. When I appeared before the Council, I was told: they advised me to absent myself for a while, so that they would not have any unrest because of me. In response to this, I had composed my answer in writing and intended to hand it over, but the Head Pastor prevented it and demanded that no written answer be accepted from me—

<summary>Jacob Böhme describes the internal conflict within the Görlitz City Council, where some members defended his writings against the Head Pastor's demands for his imprisonment and banishment. Despite this support, the Council ultimately advised Böhme to leave the city to avoid political trouble with higher authorities.</summary>

<keywords>Jacob Böhme, Gregor Richter, Görlitz City Council, The Way to Christ, religious persecution, Elector of Saxony, banishment, Primarius</keywords>

--- Page 260 ---
<meta>This page continues Jacob Böhme’s account of his appearance before the Görlitz City Council. The text picks up mid-sentence, likely referring to the Council's refusal to formally hear Böhme’s defense or examine his writings in detail to avoid further conflict with the Lead Pastor, Gregor Richter.</meta>

...[heard], for he feared he would have to answer for his lies. Thus, my defense was not accepted by the Council; instead, I was only warned to keep myself away, or—since other people would gladly have me stay with them—to go to them, so that the Council might finally have peace.

To this, I gave them the following answer: if they did not wish to hear my response so that I might plead my innocence, and if no protection could be granted against the <term>Lead Pastor: Primarius; the chief clergyman of the city, Gregor Richter, who was Böhme's primary antagonist</term>'s agitation and unfair insults, then I must commit it to God. I would see where God might lead me among pious people and finally grant me a little place, so that I might at last be out of the Lead Pastor’s sight. This pleased the Council members, yet they issued no formal command that I should or must leave; rather, they only warned me.

With that, I departed from the Council to go home. There, before the Council doors in the outer room, stood several sharp-tongued mockers—the Lead Pastor’s followers, perhaps even sent by him—who mocked me. One among them, a base fellow, <note>original: "anatomirte" — literally "anatomized." In this context, it means he scrutinized and criticized every part of Böhme's person and character with malicious detail.</note> dissected me from the crown of my head to the soles of my feet, violently attacking and mocking my clothes and my gifts, as well as the Spirit of God. Finally, he even said: the Holy Spirit would eventually become as common as the fur scraps at the <term>furriers: Kürschner; craftsmen who make and repair fur garments</term>.

<summary>Böhme describes how the City Council, seeking peace, pressured him to leave voluntarily rather than formally judging his case, and recounts being harassed by the Lead Pastor's followers as he left the chamber.</summary>

<meta>This page follows Böhme’s description of his mistreatment at the hands of the Görlitz City Council and his decision to leave the city to avoid further conflict with Lead Pastor Gregor Richter. The catchword at the bottom right is partially cut off.</meta>

“Thus this matter came to an end, and the <term>Lead Pastor: Primarius; the head clergyman of Görlitz, Gregor Richter</term> has, moreover, had this <term>lampoon: Pasquill; a public satirical pamphlet or libelous writing</term> printed; and I must now truly stand beneath the Cross of Christ <note>To stand "beneath the Cross" is a common 17th-century expression for enduring religious persecution or suffering as a test of faith.</note>. May God bring it to pass according to His counsel. I pray that the <term>nobleman: Junker; a title for a young landowning aristocrat</term> would also indicate his opinion to me in this matter, as God grants him insight. I indeed suffer it all with patience, but my children’s reputation is shamefully defamed <note>original: "geehrenrühret" — literally "honor-wounded"; to have one's social standing and character publicly attacked</note> by it, which must nonetheless be so, so that the measure of sin may be full and the punishment may come.”

Böhme was all the more willing to leave Görlitz for a time, as he had already been invited by several distinguished men in Dresden to come to them. He therefore departed on May 9, 1624, traveling via Zittau to Dresden. From here, between May 15 and June 13, he wrote four letters to his friend, Tobias Kober, a physician in Görlitz, which follow here verbatim, though with some insignificant passages omitted.

->Dresden, May 15, 1624.<-

My very dear and worthy Sir and brother in the life and power of our Lord Jesus Christ, along with my heartfelt wishes for divine love and patience beneath the Cross of Christ. I have indeed received your writing and sensed therein your Christian, brotherly love, as it was already well known to me, and I rejoice in your health and that of my family. I have also, praise God, arrived here under favorable circumstances, together with Mr. Melchior Bernt—

<meta>This page continues a letter from Jacob Böhme to his friend, the physician Tobias Kober. Böhme has just arrived in Dresden following his self-imposed exile from Görlitz to escape the "fire" of religious persecution. He describes his warm reception by the high-ranking officials of the Saxon court.</meta>

...iced to hear of your well-being. I have **arrived at the home of Mr. Benedict Hinkelmann**, <term>His Electoral Grace’s: Referring to the Elector of Saxony, the sovereign prince of the region</term> <term>Chemist: Chymikus; in the 17th century, this often implied an alchemist or court apothecary</term> and <term>Practitioner: Practicus; a practicing physician</term>, where I have been offered every Christian love and friendship, and we spend our time daily in good conversation. 

My arrival has also been announced by Mr. Hinkelmann to nearly all the Electoral Councilors, almost all of whom read and love my printed booklet <note>Likely referring to "The Way to Christ" (Der Weg zu Christo), the only work of Böhme's published during his lifetime, which caused the controversy in Görlitz</note>, recognizing it as a divine gift and using it daily. They have also already sent their greetings and kind regards to me several times through Mr. Hinkelmann, desiring that I should tarry here, as they wish to arrange matters so that they might enter into conversation with me.

In this regard, **Mr. Joachim von Loß**, an Imperial and Electoral Councilor and a very wise gentleman, has indicated to me that at the conclusion of the holidays he intends to travel to his **Castle Pillnitz**, located one mile from Dresden. He wishes to take me and Mr. Hinkelmann there with him and keep us for several days for a good discussion. Likewise, the **House Marshal** and the **Grand Master of the Stables** are also well-disposed toward me, and I still hope that these gentlemen will not only receive me with favor but also promote several of my writings, all of which I consider to be a **divine providence** <note>original: "göttliche Schickung" — a term Böhme frequently uses to describe events orchestrated by God's will</note>. 

I shall hardly be able to return home in under three weeks, both because I must wait here and because the **fire of Satan** <note>Böhme uses this dramatic imagery to describe the intense anger and legal threats from the clergy (specifically Pastor Gregor Richter) in his hometown of Görlitz</note> is so great at home...

He views this hospitality as divine intervention and delays his return to Görlitz due to the ongoing religious conflict there.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Jacob Böhme’s letter to his friend Tobias Kober, written from Dresden in 1624. ...burns, while I have good peace here and hear nothing of such clamor. 

I exhort you in love to have patience and wait to see what God intends to do. For my part, I have no <term>melancholy: Schwermuth; in Böhme’s time, this was seen as both a physical ailment and a spiritual trial of the soul</term>, but am instead quite joyful, seeing how the devil is so enraged against me and tells such lies about me. It is the <note>original: "Christi Mahlzeichen"</note> mark of Christ’s suffering, but the slanderer’s <note>The "slanderer" refers to Pastor Gregorius Richter of Görlitz, Böhme's primary antagonist, who frequently attacked him from the pulpit</note> lies will surely come to light, so that our enemies shall be put to shame. Let us only pray and commit the judgment to God. The enemy does this to Christ and His children themselves. His excuse is false and will not hold. Would to God he prayed in such a way that he brought the Holy Spirit to the pulpit, rather than the wrathful, slandering devil. It is now the turn of the <note>original: "Hure zu Babel"</note> **Whore of Babylon** <note>A common Reformation-era term for the corrupt institutional church, referencing the Book of Revelation</note>; that is why the devil is so very enraged.

I ask that you would speak with my wife and tell her to take heart in patience and be at peace, and not to become so discouraged as I hear she is. It goes very well for me, and I am held in honor and very dearly. I will not abandon her; if we have no room in one place, God will lead us to another. For I am now watching for the Lord’s ways to see what He will do. She should not consider this a disgrace, for we are persecuted for the sake of divine knowledge and gifts, and for the sake of Christ, our Redeemer. Chri-

<meta>This page continues Jacob Böhme’s letter to his friend Tobias Kober, written from Dresden in May or June 1624. Böhme is reassuring his friend and family while he seeks protection from the Elector of Saxony against the local persecution he faced in Görlitz.</meta>

...[Chri]stus <note>original: "stus"; the beginning of the word "Christus" was likely on the preceding page</note> told us to rejoice when it goes thus for us, for our reward in heaven is great. I **hope** to still provide **well** for her and our children, if God wills; she should only remain content in patience, and let no one put ideas in her head. I will take good care of myself; she need not worry on my account. The time will also come when it will not be a burden to her. No one has anything dishonest to say about us, except for a single **evil** man, who lies about us and assails us for Christ’s **sake**. It is pure joy to me to suffer disgrace for the sake of Christ and his gift; even our enemy must help promote the good.

The threatening of our enemy is only his malicious will; who knows if a <note>An allusion to Isaiah 37:29, implying God will restrain or lead the enemy away like a beast</note> ring might not be put in his nose. I will not leave the Electoral Councilors **unaware** of this, though they already know of it and do not speak well of him for it. I also hope to come before His Electoral Grace himself, in person, and I hope everything will turn out well. The <term>Head Pastor: Primarius; the title for the lead clergyman in the city of Görlitz, Gregorius Richter</term> must not boast so much of his high status and rely on lies and on men; his "Christian heart" will surely be revealed, and even his current friends might resist him.

Because it seems wrong to him that my little book was published without a name, I hope it shall soon [be published] under a name...

On the Sunday after the Ascension of Christ <note>original: "Christi Himmelfahrt"</note>, he wrote again to Dr. Kober, including the following: 

"Among the Elector’s <term>Elector: One of the high-ranking German princes who chose the Holy Roman Emperor; here referring to Johann Georg I of Saxony</term> most distinguished councilors, there are truly Christian gentlemen to whom this <term>theosophical foundation: Böhme’s system of spiritual knowledge involving the nature of God and the universe</term> is very dear, and who love and read my writings. For my printed booklet <note>Böhme refers here to "The Way to Christ," the only work published during his lifetime</note> is in the hands of many officers and other learned men, who all recognize it as good and a gift from God. They are working toward ways to help promote such writings—which lead man into the <term>Imitation of Christ: The spiritual practice of following the life and teachings of Jesus</term>—rather than suppressing them, as is unfortunately happening in my homeland <note>Görlitz</note> due to hatred of my person.

The <term>Pasquill: A libelous or satirical lampoon</term> of the <term>Primarius: The head pastor, referring here to Böhme’s antagonist in Görlitz, Gregor Richter</term> is despised by the priests here, who say that he has entirely overstepped the bounds of his office by writing it. For Mr. Hinkelmann <note>Abraham von Hinckelmann, a court official in Dresden and supporter of Böhme</note> has shown it to the councilors and scholars, who marvel at the man’s folly in daring to pour out his <term>passions: In this context, his uncontrolled anger or biased emotions</term> in public against such a Christian booklet. Many a pious heart is horrified by it and considers it a punishment from God that those who ought to teach others are so blind and resist the truth itself.

I beg of you, sir, to please suggest to my wife that she should not worry about me, but only pray diligently; God will surely [guide things well]."

I rely on no man, but rather on the living God, and am thereby quite joyful and comforted. Who knows how things might turn out? My writings are being copied here; time will reveal everything.

Dresden is now a city of celebration, just as Prague once was, and things are proceeding magnificently. From Hungary, it is reported here almost for certain that peace has been concluded between the Emperor <note>Holy Roman Emperor Ferdinand II</note> and Gábor Bethlen <note>Prince of Transylvania and a key anti-Habsburg leader</note>, but the newspapers read quite differently. In the Netherlands, things are also still quiet. Although many people there are in preparation, one hears nothing new. Sweden recruited soldiers here eight days ago, and English recruitment also continues."

<meta>This page continues Böhme's account of his stay in Dresden in 1624. This page completes that list and describes his burgeoning support among the Saxon nobility.</meta>

Schwalbach, and the House Marshal—those noble and stern high officials serving the Elector, specifically the House Marshal, the Master of the Stables, the Lord Chamberlain, and a Councillor—were guests at my host’s home. They came specifically for my sake to confer with me, and our meeting concluded in a spirit of love, favor, and mutual understanding. They listened to me very gladly, promised me their goodwill and support, and expressed a desire to speak with me further, which I am now awaiting daily. They have also presented my printed booklet <note>Likely referring to *The Way to Christ*, the only work Böhme saw published during his lifetime</note> to the Elector <note>Johann Georg I, Elector of Saxony</note>.

On the Thursday after Pentecost, Joachim von Loß <note>A powerful Saxon nobleman and diplomat</note> sent his carriage to fetch me, along with Mr. Hinkelmann and a physician, and brought us to his castle at Pillnitz to converse with me. This gentleman is very pleased with my work and spiritual gifts; he has also promised me his goodwill and support, indicating that he wishes to recommend me to the Elector. He intends to see that I might receive some form of maintenance and peace so that I may further cultivate my talent. Even the Superintendent <note>The highest Lutheran ecclesiastical authority in the district</note>, Aegidius Strauch, is pleased with my booklet, and I hope that everything will turn out well again after the harm and persecution I have suffered.

He brings shame upon the city of Görlitz and—

<meta>This page continues the account of Jacob Böhme’s funeral in Görlitz. The previous page detailed the refusal of the Chief Pastor (Primarius) Nikolaus Thomas to perform the burial service despite orders from the Town Council and pressure from the Provincial Governor. This page describes the reluctant sermon given by the clergyman Theodorus and the early reception of Böhme's works.</meta>

Theodorus began the funeral sermon <term>Leichenpredigt</term> <note>A formal sermon delivered at a burial, usually including a biographical sketch of the deceased.</note> with a Christian introduction: He stated that he would rather have walked twenty miles to do a favor for someone else than hold this sermon, but because it had been imposed upon him by a Noble and Honorable Council, he was obliged to take it upon himself and perform it. In conclusion, he omitted the customary biography of the deceased and instead posed several questions related to Böhme’s writings and doctrines. A beautiful cross, which had been sent to him from Silesia and placed upon his grave, did not stand for long, as it was pelted with filth and smashed by his enemies. Thus, even in the grave, the good man still found no peace.

Meanwhile, his writings found approval here and there, and even a son of the Chief Pastor <term>Primarius</term> <note>Referring to Gregor Richter, Böhme's primary antagonist during his life.</note> Richter held them in high regard during his father’s lifetime. He confronted his father regarding the persecution of Böhme, and later, while serving as a merchant’s assistant in Thorn <note>The city of Toruń in modern-day Poland.</note>, he published an 8-part abridgment of Böhme’s writings at his own expense between 1650 and 1660. He organized the material under specific headings, similar to an index. However, this work is very rare, as only 100 copies were printed.

Among the opponents of Böhme’s writings, three clergymen in the territory of Württemberg were particularly prominent; however, they would soon have to regret most painfully having set themselves against Böh—

<meta>This page continues the account of Böhme’s legacy and the fate of those who opposed him. The previous page ended by introducing three prominent clergymen in Württemberg who were vocal critics of Böhme’s work.</meta>

...having zealously attacked Böhme’s writings and friends from the pulpit, in that they—according to their own statements—were immediately thereafter punished by God with illness and forced to die. Since these events are mentioned by Dr. Philipp Jacob Spener in his *Theological Considerations* <term>theologische Bedenken</term> <note>original: "theologischen Bedenken"; Philipp Jacob Spener (1635–1705) was a highly influential German theologian known as the "Father of Pietism." His "Bedenken" were authoritative pastoral responses to theological questions.</note>, and thereby appear as credible facts, they can hardly be denied, although the striking nature of these accounts can be partially tempered by conjectural explanations.

The first example of God's punishment against the enemies of Böhme and his writings is told as follows: A Württemberg Superintendent <note>A high-ranking administrative cleric in the Lutheran church.</note> named Zeller once railed most vehemently against Jacob Böhme in a Sunday sermon and was seized by the punishment of God that very same day. He could not close his eyes without beholding the most terrifying hellish figures with fear and horror; and although he did not see these dreadful spirits of hell to the same degree when his eyes were open, a terrible anxiety and trembling remained within him. He would accept no comfort from his colleagues when they sought to calm him with verses from the Holy Scriptures. He remained in this state for fourteen days and then gave up the ghost.

Two other Württemberg preachers likewise died a few days after their vehement remarks against Jacob Böhme—one of them from a profound melancholy <term>Melancholie</term> <note>In this period, "melancholy" was seen as both a physical ailment and a spiritual crisis involving deep despair or divine abandonment.</note>.

<summary>This page recounts several anecdotes of divine judgment against Böhme’s critics, specifically focusing on three Württemberg clergymen whose sudden deaths were documented by the influential theologian Philipp Jacob Spener.</summary>
<keywords>Jacob Böhme, Philipp Jacob Spener, Superintendent Zeller, divine punishment, Württemberg, religious polemics, melancholy, Pietism</keywords>

--- Page 281 ---
<meta>This page continues the account of Dr. Philipp Jacob Spener’s observations regarding the legacy of Jacob Böhme. Following the accounts of critics in Württemberg who met untimely ends, Spener here offers his personal reflections and provides a specific account of a critic in Frankfurt.</meta>

To these incidents, Dr. Spener adds the following: I confess that from this time onward, such sad occurrences have caused me much worry and moved me to stay away from this fire, at which I did not wish to get burned—especially since I cannot deny that it moved me not a little on several occasions when I saw and heard that people, as soon as they read Böhme’s <note>original: "Böhmens"</note> writings, changed noticeably in their lives, and also began to read the Holy Scriptures more diligently and with more love. In another writing, *Defense of the Just Cause against Dr. Pfeiffer* <note>original: "Rettung der gerechten Sachen wider D. Pfeiffern"; Dr. August Pfeiffer was a staunch orthodox Lutheran opponent of the Pietists.</note>, Spener recounts an example of a certain Master <term>Magister</term> <note>represented by the abbreviation "M." in the text; a traditional academic title for clergy.</note> Holzhausen in Frankfurt. This man had vehemently refuted Böhme’s writings in a printed work and, shortly thereafter, fell into consumption <term>Schwindsucht</term> <note>A historical term for a wasting disease, typically tuberculosis.</note> and a terrible anguish of conscience, from which he died a few weeks later in 1693. Holzhausen confessed publicly that, because of his statements against Böhme, he had to accuse himself of the sin against the Holy Spirit <note>In Christian theology, this refers to the "unforgivable sin" of willfully attributing the works of the Holy Spirit to the devil.</note>. "I found," he said, "in Böhme's writing *The Way to Christ* <note>original: "Weg zu Christo"; first published in 1624, this remains Böhme's most famous devotional work.</note>, so much good that my heart began to tremble, wondering if I had done the man wrong with my harsh judgment, and if I had perhaps even committed the sin against the Holy Spirit by reviling the spirit from which he wrote as a lying spirit in my public writings."

<summary>Philipp Jacob Spener expresses his caution in judging Jacob Böhme, noting how Böhme's readers often become more pious. He recounts the death of Magister Holzhausen, a critic who died in spiritual agony, fearing he had committed an unforgivable sin by attacking Böhme’s work.</summary>

<keywords>Philipp Jacob Spener, Jacob Böhme, Magister Holzhausen, Dr. Pfeiffer, The Way to Christ, Sin against the Holy Spirit, Frankfurt, consumption, repentance</keywords>

--- Page 282 ---
<meta>This page continues Dr. Philipp Jacob Spener’s nuanced reflection on the critics of Jacob Böhme. Spener is analyzing whether the tragic deaths and deathbed retractions of these opponents were genuine signs of divine judgment or symptoms of their physical illnesses.</meta>

It is very well possible that a certain connection existed between the zeal of these men and their <unclear>sad</unclear> fates, although a predisposition to such <unclear>illnesses</unclear> may have already been present in them. Zealous opposition to false teachers <term>Irrlehrer</term> <note>literally "error-teachers," referring to those promoting heresy or heterodox views.</note>, when done with a pure intention and in the Spirit of God, cannot be punishable at all. Such zeal against false teachers can only be sinful if it happens without love and without the necessary examination and insight; in such a state of blindness and passion, one might blaspheme and suppress even that which is innocent, or perhaps even that which is true and divine—just as happened with the Pharisees and Scribes <term>Schriftgelehrte</term> <note>In the New Testament, these were the professional interpreters of the Law who were often portrayed as the primary opponents of Jesus.</note> in the case of Jesus. 

The violent zeal of these men against Böhme <note>original: "Böhmen"; the author uses the inflected German form of the name Jacob Böhme.</note> may have stirred up the "matter of their illness" <term>Krankheitsstoff</term> <note>A historical medical concept suggesting that diseases were caused by physical substances or "humors" in the body that could be agitated by strong emotions.</note> and thereby made them susceptible to the feeling that their zeal was unlawful. Despite all this, their fates could still have been a punishment from God <note>original: "Strafe Gottes"</note>, namely if these men had truly been zealous out of mere passion; however, one cannot sufficiently judge this. For although they declared themselves guilty for this reason, their illnesses were of such a nature that the diseases themselves might have played a greater part in these self-accusations than the actual wrongdoing of their exaggerated zeal.

Böhme's writings would altogether only ever have been [preserved] by a few of his admirers in Silesia <note>A historical region in Central Europe, mostly in modern-day Poland, where Jacob Böhme lived and worked.</note> <unclear>and</unclear>

<summary>Spener explores the psychological and physical causes behind the deaths of Böhme’s critics, questioning if their terminal remorse was a result of divine punishment or simply the delirium of their illnesses. He cautions that while zeal against heresy is not inherently sinful, it becomes so when driven by blind passion rather than love and careful study.</summary>
<keywords>Jacob Böhme, Philipp Jacob Spener, religious zeal, heresy, divine punishment, medical history, conscience, Silesia, Pharisees</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the historical account of how Jacob Böhme’s writings were preserved and disseminated. The previous page discussed the spiritual state and fate of Böhme’s critics; this page shifts focus to the physical manuscripts and the early efforts to publish his work after his death.</meta>

...had been copied in Upper Lusatia <term>Oberlausitz</term> <note>A historical region in the borderlands of modern Germany, Poland, and the Czech Republic where Böhme lived.</note>. Only in 1624 did Abraham von Frankenberg <note>A Silesian nobleman and mystic who was Böhme’s friend and first biographer.</note> have the small books *On Repentance* and *On True Resignation* printed in Görlitz under the title: *The Way to Christ* <note>original: "Weg zu Christo"</note>. Ten years later, an abridgment of the *Aurora* <note>Böhme's first major work, which had previously circulated only in manuscript form due to censorship.</note> was published in Germany, followed by several other small writings in Dresden, Halberstadt, Büdingen, and Berlin. Only in Amsterdam, from 1635 to 1704, were Böhme’s collected works published gradually in both High German and Dutch, in duodecimo, octavo, and quarto <term>Duodez, Octav und Quart</term> <note>These terms refer to the physical size of the books, based on how many times the printer's sheet of paper was folded.</note>. 

This occurred because a merchant there, Abraham Wilhelms van Beyerland, happened to come across a work by Böhme, and it made such a profound impression on him that he wrote to Böhme’s friends in Upper Lusatia in order to obtain all of his writings. The lawyer Hans Rothen von Baumgarten possessed the complete set in transcriptions and sold them to Beyerland for 100 Reichsthaler <term>Reichsthaler</term> <note>The standard silver currency of the Holy Roman Empire.</note>. However, to the great terror of the buyer, the wagon carrying the manuscripts was plundered during the wartime unrest of those days <note>A reference to the Thirty Years' War (1618–1648), which devastated Central Europe.</note>, and he already believed them lost when, contrary to all expectations, he received them—the looters had simply left the manuscripts lying there, regarding them as something useless. Following this, he continued to purchase even more copies and several original manuscripts from various sources,

<summary>This page recounts the early publication history of Jacob Böhme’s works, detailing how his manuscripts survived the Thirty Years' War and were eventually published in Amsterdam by Abraham Wilhelms van Beyerland.</summary>
<keywords>Jacob Böhme, Abraham von Frankenberg, The Way to Christ, Aurora, Amsterdam, Abraham Wilhelms van Beyerland, Hans Rothen von Baumgarten, book history, Thirty Years' War</keywords>

--- Page 284 ---
<meta>This page continues the historical account of the publication and translation of Jacob Böhme’s works. The previous page ended with the merchant Abraham Wilhelms van Beyerland acquiring Böhme's manuscripts despite the dangers of wartime unrest.</meta>

...in order to be able to correct the text as much as possible. Then he translated them into Dutch and published them individually. In the following period, some writings were also translated into Dutch and French, and the *Forty Questions on the Soul* <note>original: "vierzig Fragen von der Seele"</note> were even translated into Latin. In England, however, the complete writings were translated into English and published in print from 1661 to 1662.

The first complete edition of all of Böhme’s writings <note>The German text uses the adjective "Böhmischen," referring to Jacob Böhme.</note> in the High German language appeared in 1682 in Amsterdam. It was prepared by Johann Georg Gichtel, a legal scholar from Speyer who, after various strokes of fate, had come to Amsterdam and remained there until his death in 1710. A God-fearing mayor and friend of Gichtel provided the funds for this edition. To precisely correct the previous printing of Böhme's writings, he purchased all the manuscripts from Beyerland's heirs and also took his countryman, Georg Christian Fuchs, into his home so that he could assist him with this comparison and correction. After the printing of the work was completed, he added insightful marginal notes <term>Marginalien</term> <note>Notes written in the margins of a book, often providing commentary or clarification.</note> to his manual. In the year 1715, the work was published again, and Gichtel’s marginal notes, along with several improvements, were included in the printing. However, when the work was published for the third time in 1730, Gichtel’s marginal notes were condensed and placed at the beginning of each chapter as a summary of contents; several corrections from Gichtel’s own personal copy <note>original: "Handexemplar," referring to the specific volume Gichtel kept and annotated by hand.</note> were also incorporated into the text.

Although Böhme’s writings contain much that is mystical, incomprehensible, and untenable, and through the countless repetitions—

<summary>This page traces the publication history of Jacob Böhme's works, specifically the efforts of Johann Georg Gichtel and his patrons in Amsterdam to produce the first complete, corrected German editions in the late 17th and early 18th centuries.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the evaluation of Jacob Böhme’s writings. While the previous page discussed the publication history and Johann Georg Gichtel’s editorial work, this page shifts to a philosophical and psychological analysis of Böhme’s work, specifically contrasting his practical religious ethics with his mystical theories.</meta>

...because of their mystical revelations, they can become exceedingly burdensome and off-putting; nevertheless, one also encounters everywhere highly attractive, edifying, luminous, and noteworthy passages. Even the mystical and untenable explanations bear the mark of a religious spirit and are interwoven here and there with poignant religious truths, even if one does not quite know how they fit into the context. However much Böhme may wax enthusiastic in theoretical matters, not a trace of such "fanaticism" <note>The original German word *Schwärmerei* refers to excessive, often irrational religious enthusiasm or "visionary" dreaming.</note> is to be found in his practical teachings. *) Just as Kant insists unconditionally on following the moral law <term>Moralgesetz</term> <note>A central concept in Immanuel Kant’s philosophy, referring to the inherent duty to act according to universal ethical principles.</note>, so Böhme insists without exception on following the will of God, on life in God. Therefore, he is also an enemy of a "dead faith in the letter" <note>original: "todten Buchstabenglauben," referring to a rigid, literal adherence to scripture that lacks internal spiritual life.</note> and of everything merely mechanical in religion. His insight into the divine, the moral, and the religious runs deep; he grasps it directly through inner intuition <term>innere Anschauung</term>, holding fast to it everywhere. He requires no motivation for right belief and right action other than the will of God present within his own soul. It is therefore no surprise that Böhme’s writings are so attractive to religious people who possess a developed faculty of understanding. They feel the truth and divinity within them deeply; however, the mystical and groundless elements do not strike them as odd, because they cannot judge them. They simply extract sustenance for their spirit from individual words and concepts that appear within the mystical passages, interpreting them according to their own strength and ability without brooding over them,

---

*) Namely in the religion of pure reason <term>Vernunft-Religion</term>; regarding certain positive religious truths, however, his expression is also somewhat mystical.

<summary>This page analyzes Jacob Böhme’s dual nature as a writer, noting that while his mystical theories can be obscure, his practical religious ethics are deeply grounded in "inner intuition" and a devotion to God's will comparable to Kantian duty.</summary>
<keywords>Jacob Böhme, Immanuel Kant, moral law, will of God, inner intuition, fanaticism, religious practice, mysticism, religion of reason</keywords>

--- Page 286 ---
<meta>This page continues the psychological and stylistic analysis of Jacob Böhme’s work. It moves from a comparison with Kantian ethics to an exploration of how Böhme’s personal character and his social standing as a "poor shoemaker" shaped the reception and nature of his mystical visions.</meta>

...and thereby to fall into errors. Böhme’s style is at times beautiful, melodious, and original, and many of its imperfections belong to the era in which he lived. His mystical concepts had become a fixed idea for him, and whenever he breaks away from them, he speaks as a man of deep reason and firm character. 

When one considers how—almost entirely without internal or external education, and without resources—he sought as a poor shoemaker to achieve certainty and stability in religion and the salvation of his soul through mere struggle, reflection, and prayer, one certainly cannot deny him respect. And if, in the extraordinary exertion of his spirit, he perceived imaginative constructs <note>original: "Phantasiegebilde," referring to the vivid mental images or "visions" Böhme experienced during his mystical trances.</note> and took them for revelations from God, one cannot hold that against him either, since he never sought or desired anything other than divine knowledge and revelations. 

His constructs of imagination and mysticism had sprung from this striving for divine knowledge; and since they do not contradict practical religious truths, but merely explain the manner of the objective being and becoming of religious subjects, it was only natural that he regarded not only the hard-won higher religious insights but also the imaginative constructs that arose alongside them as divine revelations. He held everything that he recognized—or believed he recognized—as one enlightened by God to be the product of such a revelation. Therefore, he also placed a high value on everything he wrote, for it was not he himself who spoke and wrote, but God in him and through him. Likewise, he also considered his knowledge of the **language of nature** <term>Natursprache</term> <note>A central concept in Böhme's thought, referring to a "lost" primordial language where the sounds of words perfectly correspond to the inner essence or "signature" of the things they name.</note> to be a gift from God, though evidence suggests...

<summary>This page offers a sympathetic psychological defense of Jacob Böhme, arguing that his mystical visions were the natural result of a pious, uneducated mind seeking divine truth. It highlights his belief that his writings were not his own work, but direct revelations from God, including his understanding of a "language of nature."</summary>
<keywords>Jacob Böhme, mysticism, divine revelation, shoemaker, language of nature, religious psychology, spiritual enlightenment</keywords>

--- Page 287 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of Jacob Böhme’s intellectual style, transitioning from his personal character to a critique of his linguistic theories and his profound insights into the human will.</meta>

...teaches that the explanations he provided according to the "language of nature" <note>original: "Natursprache." Böhme believed that the sounds of words, when spoken, revealed the inner essence or "signature" of the things they named.</note> are sometimes mere wordplay, and sometimes arbitrary combinations of concepts that have no connection at all to the word or syllable being explained. Biblical, astronomical, chemical, and Latin words provide him with the material for the development of his system. His depictions of Creation, the fall of Lucifer, Paradise, the first humans, and other similar subjects are mixed together from such words, enlivened here and there by hints of true, and perhaps even beautiful and grand ideas, and connected into a seemingly cohesive whole through the addition of moral and religious perspectives, relationships, and applications.

He appears in quite a different light when he tears himself away from his mystical imaginings and enters the realm of religion and the supersensible. His ideas about the divine within man, about heaven and hell, and about several other metaphysical subjects are certainly not the worst that could be said on the matter. Above all, he gained deep insights into the faculty of the will, portraying it as the source of all divine knowledge, faith, life, and action. Had his views on this been heeded, they could have had a beneficial effect on the moral and religious sciences. But out of prejudice and contempt, people saw only the <term>Schwärmer: A derogatory term used in the 17th and 18th centuries for visionaries or religious "enthusiasts" who were perceived as irrational or over-excited.</term> and failed to see at the same time the wise, religious, childlike, and principled man in Jacob Böhme. And so his writings remained food only for a few good-natured, weak, and visionary people who contented themselves with feelings and obscure mystical notions, and for whom the higher and more luminous insights in Böhme’s writings were lost.

<summary>This page critiques Böhme's "language of nature" as arbitrary while praising his profound psychological insights into the human will as the foundation of faith and action. It laments that Böhme was dismissed as a mere fanatic, causing his more sophisticated religious ideas to be overlooked by serious scholars.</summary>

<keywords>Jacob Böhme, language of nature, Creation, Lucifer, Paradise, mysticism, faculty of the will, Schwärmer, religious philosophy, metaphysics</keywords>

<meta>This page provides a chronological and thematic index of the works of Jacob Böhme (1575–1624), following the previous page's discussion of his controversial reputation and his unique philosophical terminology.</meta>

### List of all Böhme’s Writings. <note>original: "Böhmischen Schriften." While "Böhmisch" usually means "Bohemian," in this context it refers specifically to the works of Jacob Böhme.</note>

1. *Aurora, or the Morning Redness.*
2. *Of the Three Principles*, with an Appendix.
3. *Of the Threefold Life of Man.*
4. *Forty Questions on the Soul*, along with the *Inward Eye*. <note>The German "ungewandten Auge" is likely a transcription error for "umgewandten Auge," a term Böhme used to describe the soul's focus turning back toward God.</note>
5. *On the Incarnation of Jesus Christ.*
6. *Of Six Theosophical Points.*
7. *Of Six Mystical Points.*
8. *Of the Earthly and Heavenly Mystery.*
9. *The Way to Christ*, in eight books, namely: 1. *Of True Repentance*. 2. *Of Holy Prayer*. 3. *Of True Resignation*. <note>original: "Gelassenheit." This is a central concept in German mysticism, referring to a state of letting go of self-will to be filled by God.</note> 4. *Of the New Regeneration*. 5. *Of the Supersensual Life*. 6. *Of Divine Contemplation*. 7. *Dialogue between an Enlightened and an Unenlightened Soul*. 8. *Of the Four Complexions*. <note>Refers to the four classical temperaments: choleric, melancholic, sanguine, and phlegmatic.</note>
10. Two Apologies against Balthasar Tilke. <note>Tilke was a contemporary critic who accused Böhme of heresy.</note>
11. Considerations on Esaias Stiefel's Booklet, and on the Error of the Sects of Esaias Stiefel and Ezechiel Meth.
12. Defense against Gregorius Richter, along with the written response to the Honorable Council of Görlitz. <note>Richter was the primary pastor in Böhme’s hometown of Görlitz and a fierce opponent of his writings.</note>
13. Instruction on the Last Times to Paul Kaym.
14. *On the Birth and Signature of All Beings.* <note>Also known by its Latin title, "De Signatura Rerum." It argues that all things in nature bear a physical "signature" of their inner essence.</note>
15. *On the Election of Grace.*
16. *On the Sacraments of Christ.*
17. Explanation of the First Book of Moses, with a Short Abstract, or *The Great Mystery*. <note>original: "Mysterium Magnum." This is Böhme’s massive commentary on the book of Genesis.</note>
18. *Contemplation of Divine Revelation.*
19. Tables of the Three Principles of Divine Revelation.
20. Explanation of the Principal Points and Words in these Writings.
21. Theosophical Epistles.

<summary>This page lists twenty-one major works by the mystic Jacob Böhme, ranging from his early "Aurora" to his late theological defenses and his massive commentary on Genesis.</summary>
<keywords>Jacob Böhme, bibliography, Aurora, Mysterium Magnum, The Way to Christ, Görlitz, theosophy, seventeenth-century mysticism</keywords>

34. **Forgiveness of sins and divine service.** <note>original: "Gottesdienst." In Böhme's time, this refers both to formal church liturgy and the internal spiritual service of the heart.</note> *Of the Incarnation of Jesus Christ* <note>original: "Menschw."</note> 2, 7, 16—17. *The Threefold Life of Man* <note>original: "Dreif. Leb."</note> 11, 106. Chapter 14, 8—10. Chapter 11, 63—64. 66.

35. **Punishment and reward in and after death.** *The Three Principles of the Divine Essence* <note>original: "Drei Princ."</note> 19, 43—46. *The Threefold Life of Man* 18, 9—11.

36. **Temptation, tribulation, and doubt.** <note>original: "Anfechtung, Trübsal und Zweifel." "Anfechtung" specifically refers to the spiritual trials or "assaults" the soul feels when distanced from God.</note> *Of the Incarnation of Jesus Christ* 3, 8, 4—7. *The Threefold Life of Man* 14, 39. 41.

37. **Poor upbringing of children.** *The Threefold Life of Man* 15, 4—6. 14. 17—18.

38. **Labor, greed, and trust in God.** *The Threefold Life of Man* 17, 4. 6—10. 12—13.

39. **The Holy Supper.** <note>original: "Das heilige Abendmahl," referring to the Eucharist or Communion.</note> *Christian Testament of Holy Baptism* <note>original: "Chr. Test. d. Taufe."</note> I, 1, 1. 12. 14—17. *The Threefold Life of Man* 14, 10. *The Three Principles of the Divine Essence* 23, 3. 5. 8—11. 13. Chapter 25, 89. 92. *Christian Testament of the Holy Supper* <note>original: "Ch. Test. d. Abendm."</note> 3, 1—5. 42—46.

40. **The Spirit, not merely the letter, must speak from the teacher of religion.** <note>A common Böhmean theme: true theology comes from divine inspiration rather than academic study alone.</note> *The Great Mystery* <note>original: "Myst. Magn." or "Mysterium Magnum."</note> 28, 52. 54. 56—57. *Reflections on Stiefel* <note>original: "Bed. Stief."</note> 100—101. 111. 115—117. *The Election of Grace* <note>original: "Gnadenwahl."</note> 12, 64.

41. **New teachings and perspectives.** *The Three Principles of the Divine Essence* 27, 22—35.

42. **Bad morals among many scholars and superiors.** *The Threefold Life of Man* 15, 7—9.

43. **Origin of religious sects.** *The Three Principles of the Divine Essence* 26, 15. 23. 27. 28.

44. **Despisers of the German language.** <note>Böhme defended writing his complex theology in German rather than the scholarly Latin favored by the university-trained "reason-learned" clergy.</note> *Aurora* <note>original: "Morgenröthe." Böhme's first work, describing the "dawning" of divine knowledge.</note> 10, 27—28. 30—32.

45. **God works more through the lowly than through the high.** *Aurora* 9, 1—11. 21.

46. **Eternal supersensible being, willing, and creating of God.** *Of Earthly and Heavenly Mystery* <note>original: "Ird. u. himml. Myst."</note> Text. 1—4.

47. **What is God within Himself, apart from nature and creation?** <note>This explores the "Ungrund," the groundless or bottomless nature of God before the manifestation of the physical or spiritual worlds.</note> *Theosophical Questions* <note>original: "Theosoph. Fr."</note> 1—3. *The Election of Grace* 1, 27. Chapter 2, 3. *The Threefold Life of Man* 5, 51. *The Great Mystery* 29, 1.

48. **The unfathomable nature of the soul's origin.** *Forty Questions on the Soul* <note>original: "Vierz. Fr."</note> 269—276. *The Three Principles of the Divine Essence* 7, 6.

49. **Creation of the Sun, Mars, and Jupiter.** <note>Böhme’s cosmology often links the seven planets to the seven "source-spirits" or qualities of divine manifestation.</note> *The Three Principles of the Divine Essence* <note>Note: The OCR says "Drei Prim.", likely a typo for "Drei Princ."</note> 8, 20—24. *Aurora* 25, 68—72. 75—76. 81—82.

50. **The Creation.** *The Signature of All Things* <note>original: "Bez. d. Dinge" or "Signatura Rerum."</note> 16, 1—4. *Reflections on Stiefel* 31. *Explanation of the Principal Points* <note>original: "Erkl. d. vornehmst. Punkte."</note> 128.

<summary>This page lists index entries 34 through 50 of Jacob Böhme’s works, covering topics from social ethics (child-rearing, greed) and church sacraments to his core mystical doctrines on the nature of God, the soul, and the creation of the cosmos.</summary>
<keywords>Forgiveness, Holy Supper, religious sects, German language, supersensible being, Ungrund, soul's origin, cosmology, Aurora, Jacob Böhme</keywords>

--- Page 292 ---
<meta>This page concludes the thematic index of Jacob Böhme’s works, moving from the previous page's focus on practical piety and the Holy Supper into more complex metaphysical subjects like the fall of Lucifer, the pre-fallen state of Adam, and the mystical "Language of Nature." It also lists the history and distribution of Böhme’s own writings.</meta>

51. **Lucifer’s beauty, rebellion, and fall.** *The Aurora* <note>original: "Morgenröthe." Böhme's first major work, symbolizing the "dawn" of spiritual knowledge.</note> 13, 92–93. 105. Chapter 15, 9–11. 28–34. Chapter 19, 117.

52. **Adam before the fall.** *The Three Principles of the Divine Essence* <note>original: "Drei Princ."</note> 10, 17–20. *The Great Mystery* <note>original: "Myst. Magn." for "Mysterium Magnum," Böhme's vast commentary on Genesis.</note> 18, 10. 12–14.

53. **Unity and disunity in the will.** *The Threefold Life of Man* <note>original: "Dreif." or "Dreifaltiges Leben."</note> 13, 31–34.

54. **Origin of organized bodies.** *The Aurora.* 8, 43–62.

55. **The seventh petition explained according to the Language of Nature.** <note>The "seventh petition" refers to the final part of the Lord's Prayer: "Deliver us from evil." Böhme often analyzed the phonetic sounds of words to find their hidden spiritual power, a concept he called "Natursprache" or the Language of Nature.</note> *The Threefold Life of Man.* 16, 37–43.

56. **The word "Spoke": explained according to the Language of Nature.** <note>original: "Das Wort Sprach." This likely refers to the creative "speaking" of God in Genesis.</note> *The Aurora.* 18, 88–125.

57. **Explanation of the Trinity of God.** <note>original: "Dreieinigkeit."</note> *The Aurora.* 3, 13–28.

58. **Indication of the properties of things through external designation.** <note>This refers to the "Signature of All Things," the idea that the outer form of a plant, mineral, or person reveals its inner spiritual essence.</note> *The Signature of All Things* <note>original: "Bez. d. Dinge" or "De Signatura Rerum."</note> 1. 8. 5–7. 9. 11–16.

59. **Sickness, medicine, and physician.** *The Great Mystery.* 18, 15. Chapter 13, 53–54. 56–59. Chapter 2, 5. Chapter 6, 20–23. *The Aurora.* 21, 112–115.

60. **Origin and distribution of Jacob Böhme’s writings.** <note>These references track how Böhme’s manuscripts were written and shared during his life, often in secret to avoid persecution.</note> *The Aurora.* 19, 5–14. Chapter 14, 39–43. Chapter 25, 5–10. 50–51. *Theosophical Letters.* <note>original: "Theosoph. Briefe."</note> 12, 9–19. Letter 2, 10. Letter 34, 7–8. Letter 10, 30–31. Letter 21, 4–10. Letter 35, 14–19. Letter 50, 9. *The Three Principles of the Divine Essence.* 24, 1. *The Signature of All Things.* 9, 60. 64. *Errors of Stiefel* <note>original: "Irrth. Stief." refers to Böhme's polemical work against the sectarian Esaias Stiefel.</note> 307. *The Aurora.* 11, 71–72.
